Application- and Hardware-Aware Backbones for Fine-Grained
Image Recognition with Teacher-Guided Data Augmentation

Abstract

65

8

Fine-grained image recognition (FGIR) on phones and embedded
devices runs under a fixed compute budget, but the most accurate models are large vision transformers pretrained on ImageNet.
This paper presents teacher-guided data augmentation (TGDA): a
counterfactual attention learning (CAL) teacher’s part attention
module drives per-image cropping and dropping augmentations,
and the same frozen teacher distils into the student. ViTFS is a vision
transformer whose components and normalisation are each chosen
for mobile inference. The student never sees ImageNet: training
on CUB processes 120 million images, 96 percent of which is the
teacher. At 448×448, ViTFS-T with TGDA beats a pretrained DeiT-B
(ImageNet-1k) by 1.9 and 1.6 percentage points on Aircraft and Cars,
and beats a DeiT-T trained from scratch under a prior from-scratch
recipe by 31.9 percentage points, averaged over the three datasets.
Replacing LayerNorm with BatchNorm speeds up on-device inference by 2.64 times at 224 × 224 on the Samsung Galaxy A53 5G,
and is slower than LayerNorm on flagship system-on-chips at that
size. The recipe loses to a counterfactual attention baseline on two
soybean datasets with a convolutional student, a scope bound tied
to part localisability at the student’s resolution. The deployed model
is trained on the target dataset only; the pretrained model is the
teacher, which is not shipped.

Figure 1: Attention used for augmentation, from a ResNet101 teacher fine-tuned on CUB with counterfactual attention
learning at 448 × 448. The first row holds the original images,
the second holds the class activation maps (CAMs) that prior
work uses [12, 35], and the last two rows hold two maps
sampled from the part attention module (PAM) for the same
images.

Keywords

fine-grained image recognition, knowledge distillation, data augmentation, vision transformers, on-device inference

precision agriculture, and wildlife monitoring, where the camera
is a phone or an embedded sensor rather than a workstation GPU.
These settings fix the compute budget and often the input resolution, and low-resolution capture removes exactly the fine detail
FGIR depends on to separate visually similar categories. An Arm
Ethos-U65 microcontroller [1] and an ESP32-S3 [7] carry only a
few megabytes of memory, and the mobile system-on-chips Qualcomm AI Hub profiles [20] run inference under a latency budget
set by the surrounding application. The model has to run inside
that budget on the device itself, after whatever training happened
elsewhere, and it has to do so at a resolution the application dictates
rather than the resolution the model was designed for. Meeting that
budget while keeping FGIR’s fine inter-class discrimination is the
deployment problem this paper addresses.
The FGIR models that reach the highest accuracy are large vision transformers pretrained on ImageNet-1k [5] or ImageNet-21k
[6, 22, 26] and then fine-tuned on the target dataset; their accuracy
is inseparable from that pretraining. A model small enough for
a phone loses much of that accuracy when trained from scratch
on the target dataset alone, because FGIR datasets are small and
a compact model with no prior visual knowledge overfits them

Introduction

Fine-grained image recognition (FGIR) distinguishes subordinate
categories within a broader class, such as bird species [28] or car
models [11]. Deployment settings for FGIR include retail shelf audit,

initialisation, or initialised by a contrastive or self-supervised pretext task, trailing pretrained accuracy by tens of percentage points
at 224 × 224. Shrinking a pretrained architecture to fit a phone
does not remove this dependence; it only shrinks the model that
still needs the pretraining. It also inherits design choices made for
ImageNet-scale data and GPU inference rather than for a small
target dataset and a mobile runtime. The pretraining step itself
cannot run on the device that will run the model.
The recipe this paper proposes still relies on a pretrained model,
but only during training, and that distinction matters for deployment: the pretraining cost is paid once, centrally, and never travels
to the device. The teacher is a torchvision ResNet-101 [19] pretrained on ImageNet-1k for 90 epochs and then fine-tuned on the
target dataset with counterfactual attention learning (CAL) [21]
for 50 epochs; the deployed student never sees ImageNet. For CUB,
training processes 120 million images in total, of which the teacher’s
pretraining and fine-tuning account for 96 percent and the student’s
own training the remaining 4.8 million. That budget is about an
order of magnitude fewer images than an ImageNet-21k-pretrained
ViT-B [6, 22] processes, and roughly three times fewer than an
ImageNet-1k-pretrained DeiT-B [26] processes. A large teacher is
still trained; it is not shipped.
The training regime above raises two design questions: how the
teacher’s knowledge should reach a student of a different resolution
and device, and how a compact architecture should be shaped
without inheriting one from a pretrained checkpoint. This paper
makes the following contributions.

is the best training setting except for a ResNet-18 student on the
two soybean datasets, where a counterfactual-attention teacher
without data-aware augmentation wins; that reversal is a scope
bound, not noise (Table 7). The distillation weight and temperature
sweeps confirm the main configuration sits near its optimum rather
than under it (Table 9, Table 10). Every accuracy cell behind these
findings is a single run, one seed per configuration.

2 Related Work
2.1 Fine-grained image recognition
Fine-grained image recognition (FGIR) classifies subordinate categories within a broader class, such as bird species or car models, where inter-class differences are small and intra-class variation is large. Standard benchmarks cover birds, aircraft, and cars
[11, 15, 28]. The dominant recipe pairs a large backbone pretrained
on a generic dataset with a mechanism that localises discriminative parts, whether through learned attention or class activation
maps. Counterfactual attention learning (CAL) [21] trains such an
attention mechanism with counterfactual causality. It compares a
prediction under the learned attention map against a prediction under a counterfactual, non-informative map. Attention is rewarded
for changing that prediction, not merely for correlating with it. This
paper uses a CAL-trained model as its teacher.
A large-scale study of FGIR training and evaluation settings
reports that backbone choice and the training and evaluation protocol both drive the accuracy-cost trade-off. It also reports that
data-aware augmentation during training can recover most of the
accuracy that would otherwise require an inference-time crop [23].
Separately from FGIR, work on convolutional building blocks for
low-resolution images and small objects has established that removing strided convolutions and pooling layers, which discard spatial
detail early, improves accuracy when the input is small (Sunkara
and Luo [25]). This paper’s convolutional stem for low-resolution
inputs follows that same finding rather than superseding it.

• Teacher-guided data augmentation (TGDA): a CAL teacher’s
part attention module (PAM) produces per-image attention
maps that drive attention cropping and attention dropping,
and the same frozen teacher distils into the student through
knowledge distillation (KD).
• ViTFS: a vision transformer whose components – convolutional stem, positional encoding, LayerScale, register tokens
– are each selected against a measurement rather than carried over from ImageNet-scale design. Its normalisation
is chosen for the mobile runtime that executes it, not for
training convenience.
• An evaluation that reports accuracy, inference cost and ondevice latency together, across eleven devices, rather than
accuracy alone, so a gain in one is never read as free in the
others.
• A scope bound the evaluation exposes: TGDA’s gain over
a fine-grained-aware teacher depends on how localisable
object parts are at the student’s resolution, stated plainly
rather than left implicit.

2.2

Knowledge distillation and task-specific
augmentation

Knowledge distillation (KD) trains a smaller student network to
match the output distribution of a larger teacher [9]. FGIR-specific
variants add augmentation driven by the teacher’s own attention:
CEKD [35] cross-distils between augmented views of an ensemble,
and a dynamic semantic-structure distillation method [12] distils
high-resolution teacher structure into a low-resolution student.
Alongside this line, generic augmentation policies transform inputs
without any teacher signal: TrivialAugment [18] samples a single
random operation per image, CutMix [33] and Mixup [34] combine pairs of images and labels, and Random Erasing [37] masks
a random region. Marrie et al. [16] study task-specific distillation
from large pretrained visual models. They report that established
distillation practice needs revisiting for such teachers, and that a
diffusion-based Mixup variant complements standard augmentation
when downstream samples are scarce. Their setting is distillation
from a generically pretrained teacher rather than a fine-grainedspecialised one.

At 448 × 448, ViTFS-T trained with TGDA exceeds the ImageNet1k-pretrained DeiT-B on Aircraft and Cars while training on a far
smaller corpus, and trails the ImageNet-21k-pretrained baselines
on CUB (Table 4). Against other efficient vision transformers it
reaches comparable accuracy at a smaller parameter and FLOP
count (Table 5). Replacing LayerNorm with BatchNorm is faster
at 448 × 448 on most of the eleven measured devices, and slower
at 224 × 224 on flagship system-on-chips (Table 3); the proposed
mechanism is the absence of an accelerated LayerNorm kernel on

TGDA sits in the attention-driven augmentation line but departs
from it in what supplies the attention. It reuses the part attention
module of its FGIR teacher directly, rather than the class activation maps that CEKD and prior FGIR-KD methods derive post hoc.
TGDA also couples augmentation and distillation to one frozen
teacher, so the same attention that generates the augmented view
also supervises the student’s prediction on it.

the teacher, so its attention is shaped by the same objective that
produces the teacher’s predictions.
The frozen teacher supervises stage two. Given an image, the
teacher and the student each produce predictions on the original
image and on its augmented views. A crop or a drop can remove the
evidence a label depends on, so only the original image is supervised
against the ground-truth label; the augmented views are supervised
only through the teacher. The student trains against the loss in

2.3

Efficient vision transformers and on-device
inference

A separate line designs vision transformers (ViTs) for inference
cost rather than for accuracy alone. EfficientViT [13] restructures
attention for memory efficiency, LeViT [8] and MobileViT [17]
hybridise convolutional and attention stages for faster inference,
and Swin Transformer [14], PVT [29], NesT [36], and TinyViT [30]
build hierarchical or distilled variants that trade representational
capacity for lower cost. These families optimise throughput or
FLOPs on generic image classification, not FGIR.
ViTFS (Section 4) draws individual components from this literature rather than proposing new ones. A convolutional stem replaces
the linear patch embedding [31]; LayerScale [27] and register tokens [4] stabilise training and remove high-norm artifact tokens;
and BatchNorm [10] replaces LayerNorm [2] in the transformer
blocks. Normalisation choice in transformers has been studied for
reasons beyond accuracy, including runtime behaviour on hardware not designed for LayerNorm’s per-token statistics [24]. What
is missing from the efficient-ViT literature is a cost account that
matches how FGIR is deployed. These families are evaluated at ImageNet scale under large-scale pretraining, and their reported cost is
FLOPs rather than latency measured on the mobile and embedded
devices FGIR models actually run on.

𝑦𝑆 and 𝑦𝑇 are student and teacher predictions on the original
(og) and augmented (aug) views, 𝑦gt is the ground-truth label, LCE
is cross-entropy, and LKD is Kullback-Leibler divergence at temperature 𝑇 . The runs in this paper use a KD term without the 𝑇 2 factor

The pool PAM samples from is a knob between 1 and 8 maps
per image (Table 11). A larger pool adds teacher computation per
training step and buys accuracy: widening it from 1 to 8 maps
gains 4.4 pp on Aircraft. This cost is paid only during training; at
inference the student runs alone, without the teacher or PAM, so
the map count is a training-budget decision, not a deployment one.
TGDA is agnostic to the student’s architecture; Section 4 designs a
student architecture for on-device inference and trains it with the
recipe described here.

Teacher-Guided Data Augmentation (TGDA)
4

TGDA trains a student from scratch on the target dataset in two
stages, shown in Figure 2. A teacher is trained first and then frozen;
it then supervises the student with KD on the original image and on
augmented views the teacher itself generates. Training the teacher
first, rather than jointly with the student, lets the augmentation
depend on parts the teacher has already learned to localise on the
target dataset, instead of a fixed, hand-designed schedule.
The teacher is a torchvision ResNet-101 pretrained on ImageNet1k and fine-tuned on the target dataset with CAL [21]. CAL trains
a part attention module (PAM) on top of the backbone: a convolutional head that turns an intermediate feature map into a set of
attention maps, one per learned part. TGDA samples one map from
that set per augmentation, weighted toward the maps carrying
more attention mass, so a crop and a drop of the same image can
be guided by different parts.
The sampled map drives two augmentations. Attention cropping
zooms into the region the map highlights; attention dropping masks
that region instead, so the student sees a complementary view of
the same object. Figure 1 shows sampled PAM maps beside the
class activation maps (CAMs) that prior FGIR-KD augmentation
methods use [12, 35]; Figure 3 shows the crops and drops those maps
produce. Unlike a CAM, which is read off the classifier’s weights
after training, a PAM map comes from a head trained jointly with

Vision Transformer From Scratch (ViTFS)

Training a vision transformer (ViT) from scratch on a few thousand
fine-grained images, for inference on a phone, is a different design
problem from adapting a large pretrained ViT. The stock ViT-T/16
row of Table 1 is that problem unaddressed: without pretraining
or any of the changes below, the architecture is the starting point
ViTFS departs from. Every component ViTFS adds is taken from
prior work and evaluated here in this from-scratch, TGDA-trained,
mobile-inference setting rather than proposed as new.
The first group replaces the linear patch embedding with a convolutional stem, adds global average pooling in place of a class
token, and replaces the learned positional embedding with a twodimensional sinusoidal encoding [31]. Removing strided downsampling early in a convolutional stem is reported to help lowresolution inputs specifically [25]; ViTFS’s stem follows that same
finding. Mean over the three datasets, this group alone gains 26.4
pp at 448 × 448, relative to the stock ViT-T/16 baseline. At 224 × 224
the gain is 13.6 pp, and with the teacher at 448 × 448 and the student
at 224 × 224 it is 32.2 pp.
Adding LayerScale [27] and register tokens [4], both randomly
initialised, brings the cumulative mean gain to 28.7 pp at 448 × 448,
12.4 pp at 224 × 224, and 33.7 pp in the mixed-resolution setting.
LayerScale stabilises training by learning a per-channel scale on

Figure 2: Overview of the two-stage TGDA framework. First, a specialized teacher model is trained with part attention module
(PAM) to produce discriminative attention maps. These maps are used to generate diverse, data-aware augmentations via
attention cropping and attention dropping. Second, the frozen teacher then supervises a student model using both the original
and augmented images through knowledge distillation (KD).

is the convolutional stem with global average pooling and
a two-dimensional sinusoidal positional encoding, “LS+R”
is LayerScale with register tokens, and “BN” replaces LayerNorm with BatchNorm. The first row is a stock ViT-T/16.
Every row is trained with TGDA from random initialisation
for 800 epochs at 448 × 448 for both teacher and student, with
a ResNet-101 CAL teacher, AdamW, learning rate 5 × 10−4 ,
𝑇 = 7 and an effective KD weight of 0.2041, one seed per cell.

Replacing LayerNorm [2] with BatchNorm [10] completes ViTFS
and brings the cumulative mean gain to 30.9 pp at 448 × 448, 19.7 pp
at 224×224, and 36.6 pp in the mixed-resolution setting. BatchNorm
is adopted for two reasons, in this order: it improves accuracy, and it
removes the LayerNorm kernel that several mobile runtimes do not
accelerate. LayerNorm computes statistics per token; BatchNorm

6 Results
6.1 Latency and memory on edge devices
Does the normalisation choice that raises ViTFS-T’s accuracy also
help on the hardware it is deployed on? Some mobile inference
runtimes ship an accelerated BatchNorm kernel and no equivalent
for LayerNorm, so a kernel-level advantage would only reach practice if it survives on real devices. On the Samsung Galaxy A53 5G,
the device with the largest measured gain, replacing LayerNorm
with BatchNorm gives a 2.64× latency speedup and a 19% reduction
in peak memory at 224 × 224. At 448 × 448 the same substitution
gives a 1.21× speedup and a 24% reduction (Table 3, Figure 4). The
same substitution is not a uniform win: the speedup sits near parity
on most Pixel devices, and falls below one on flagship system-onchips at 224 × 224 and 672 × 672, where BatchNorm is slower than
LayerNorm. Peak memory follows a related but not identical pattern: BatchNorm reduces it on the Samsung Galaxy A53 5G at both
resolutions, and the reduction is likewise not uniform across the
other ten devices (Table 3). Choosing a normalisation layer for a
deployment target therefore requires measuring that target, not
assuming the kernel-level trend transfers across devices.

The backbone and the training method are chosen together. Table 2 trains ViTFS-T and a stock ViT-T/16 under CE alone and under
TGDA. Under CE, ViTFS-T already beats the stock ViT-T/16 by 62.0
pp on Cars, so the architecture change alone recovers much of what
from-scratch training otherwise loses. TGDA is not architecture
specific either: its largest gain over CE across the three datasets is
34.0 pp for the stock ViT-T/16, on Cars, and 16.3 pp for ViTFS-T, on
CUB. Neither change alone reaches what the two give together.

Experimental Setup

Experiments use three standard FGIR benchmarks: CUB [28], FGVCAircraft [15] (Aircraft in the tables) and Stanford Cars [11] (Cars in
the tables). Two ultra-fine-grained soybean-leaf datasets, SoyLocal
and SoyGene [32], appear only in the generality grid (Table 7) and
the teacher-architecture comparison (Table 8).
Three teacher/student resolution pairings recur, named consistently here and in Section 6. The 448/448 setting trains teacher and
student both at 448 × 448, used in Table 4 and Table 5. The 448/224
setting trains a 448 × 448 teacher with a 224 × 224 student, marked
“∗ ” in Table 4. The 224/224 setting trains teacher and student both
at 224 × 224, used in Table 6, Table 7 and Table 8.
The teacher is a torchvision ResNet-101 pretrained on ImageNet1k for 90 epochs, then fine-tuned on the target dataset with counterfactual attention learning (CAL) [21] for 50 epochs. The student
trains from random initialisation and never sees ImageNet. For
CUB, the pipeline processes 120 million images in total: 96% of that
is the teacher and 4.8 million images is the student.
Every student trains for 800 epochs with AdamW, learning rate
5×10−4 and weight decay 5×10−2 , one seed per cell. Every accuracy
cell in the tables that follow is a single run.
Distillation uses a temperature of 𝑇 = 7 with an effective KD
weight of 0.2041 under the normalised loss. The temperature sweep
in Table 10 instead runs at a nominal 𝛽 = 10, a different effective

6.2

Accuracy against deployment cost

Training a student from random initialisation instead of from pretrained weights raises the question of how much accuracy that
gives up, and what running the resulting model costs. If the gap
to pretrained baselines is small and the cost is low, training from
scratch is a usable alternative to pretraining rather than only a
cheaper one. ViTFS-T trained with TGDA outperforms DeiT-B pretrained on ImageNet-1k by 1.9 pp on Aircraft and 1.6 pp on Cars at
448 × 448. On CUB, the ImageNet-21k-pretrained R50-ViT-B still
leads (Table 4). Against the DeiT-T trained from scratch under the
IDMM recipe at the same resolution, ViTFS-T gains 31.9 pp, averaged over Aircraft, Cars and CUB. The Images (K) column in
Table 4 is the size of the largest training corpus each row used, so a
pretrained row reports its pretraining set and a from-scratch row
reports the target dataset’s training split.
At 224 × 224, ViTFS-T with TGDA reaches a mean top-1 accuracy of 77.6% over Aircraft, Cars and CUB, and gains 26 pp on
Cars and 37 pp on CUB over the IDMM baseline at that resolution
(Table 6). Table 5 places the 448 × 448 accuracy beside efficient
Vision Transformer baselines measured under the same conditions:
ViT-B/16 carries 15.4× as many parameters as ViTFS-T, and the

6.3

on Aircraft, Cars and CUB, and CAL with knowledge distillation
(CAL+KD) is best on SoyLocal and SoyGene instead. TGDA’s dataaware augmentation assumes discriminative parts that are spatially
localisable at the student’s resolution. SoyLocal and SoyGene are
ultra-fine-grained soybean-leaf images, and that assumption does
not hold for the ResNet-18 student on them. This is a scope bound
on the augmentation mechanism; whether the student’s resolution,
its architecture or the datasets’ part granularity causes it is not
separated by these runs. On Aircraft, Cars and CUB, TGDA is best
of the four settings for both students.

Generality across students and datasets

Do TGDA’s gains on the three standard benchmarks generalise
beyond them, and beyond one student? If the gain is specific to
Aircraft, Cars and CUB, or to the ViT-T/16 student, it would not
support a general claim about TGDA. With a ViT-T/16 student,
TGDA is the best of the four training settings on all five datasets
in Table 7, where every cell is the mean top-1 accuracy over the
six augmentation policies. With a ResNet-18 student, TGDA is best

Teacher architecture

TGDA’s teacher is a ResNet-101 in every other table. Is a convolutional teacher required? If a Vision Transformer teacher works
7

MMSys ’27, March 30–April 2, 2027, Ghent, Belgium

Anon.

6.5

813
814
815

The main tables fix the KD weight 𝛽 and the temperature 𝑇 on
CUB with a ResNet-18 student. How much of the reported accuracy
depends on that choice? If accuracy is flat over a wide range of
either constant, the specific values chosen are not doing the work.
Sweeping 𝛽 from 0.02 to 7.0 at 𝑇 = 7 peaks at 𝛽 = 0.2 with 78.70%
CUB top-1 (Table 9). The effective weight used in every other table,
0.2041, is near that peak in the normalised formulation. Sweeping 𝑇
from 0.2 to 50.0 at a nominal 𝛽 = 10 peaks at 𝑇 = 20.0 with 80.51%
(Table 10). That sweep runs at a different effective KD weight from
every other table in this paper, so its peak is not evidence that the
main configuration should have used a higher temperature. The
two sweeps do not form a single two-dimensional grid over 𝛽 and
𝑇 , because the loss scaling differs between them.
Increasing the number of attention maps drawn per image from
1 to 8 raises Aircraft top-1 accuracy by 4.4 pp (Table 11). The gain
is monotone in the map count on Aircraft and on CUB, and is not
monotone on Cars. A 32-map run exists but ran at a different KD
weight and is omitted as not comparable with these four rows.

Limitations

The training recipe requires a ResNet-101 pretrained on ImageNet1k and then fine-tuned with counterfactual attention learning (CAL);
the deployed student never sees ImageNet, but the pipeline that
produces it does. For a CUB student, the teacher accounts for 96%
of the 120 million images the pipeline processes in total. No experiment in this paper trains the teacher from scratch, so the paper does
not bound how much of the student’s accuracy comes from that
pretraining. The comparison against pretrained baselines is not a
comparison against zero pretraining; it is a comparison against a
smaller, differently sourced pretraining budget that sits upstream
of the student.
With a ResNet-18 student, TGDA is worse than CAL with knowledge distillation (CAL+KD) on SoyLocal and SoyGene (Table 7);
with a ViT-T/16 student it is not. The augmentation assumes discriminative parts that are spatially localisable at the student’s resolution.
That assumption is not met by the ResNet-18 student on these
ultra-fine-grained leaf images, whose classes separate by leaf shape
and vein pattern rather than by object parts. The paper does not
establish which of resolution, architecture or dataset granularity is
responsible for the loss.
Replacing LayerNorm with BatchNorm is not a uniform latency
win (Table 3). The LayerNorm/BatchNorm latency ratio falls below
one on flagship system-on-chips and sits near parity on most Pixel
devices, so the normalisation choice is a per-target decision, not a
default. Each cell in that table is a single measurement on one build
of the model.
Every accuracy cell in the paper is a single run. The paper reports
no variance, so small differences between rows are not distinguishable from run-to-run noise, and an ordering between two settings
that differ by a small margin is not established at this scale.
The distillation constants 𝛽 and 𝑇 were chosen on CUB, and the
sweeps that examine them (Table 9, Table 10) cover CUB with a
ResNet-18 student only. The paper does not show that the same constants are optimal for the other datasets or for the ViTFS students.

841
842

Sensitivity to the distillation
hyperparameters

cell the mean top-1 accuracy over the same six augmentation policies as Table 7. Neither teacher wins on all five datasets: RN101
leads on Cars, CUB and SoyLocal, and ViT-B/16 leads on Aircraft
and SoyGene. A convolutional teacher is not required for TGDA
to work, and which architecture is better depends on the dataset
rather than on a fixed preference for either family.

Conclusion

on the target dataset. TGDA is the best of the compared settings
for the ResNet-18 and ViT-T/16 students on the object-recognition
datasets in the generality grid (Table 7). The same augmentation
improves a ViT-T/16 student under a ResNet-101 teacher and under

Teacher-guided data augmentation (TGDA) trains fine-grained image recognition (FGIR) students that never see ImageNet, using
attention maps from a counterfactual-attention teacher fine-tuned
9

MMSys ’27, March 30–April 2, 2027, Ghent, Belgium

Anon.

a ViT-B/16 teacher alike (Table 8). Stock vision transformer students
trained with TGDA from random initialisation close most of the
gap to pretrained and from-scratch baselines (Table 6, Table 4).
ViTFS, a set of architecture changes to the vision transformer
stem and normalisation layers, narrows the accuracy gap between a
stock vision transformer student and one built for training without
pretraining (Table 2, Table 1). Adding the convolutional-stem group,
then LayerScale with register tokens, then BatchNorm raises accuracy at each step (Table 1), and the resulting student carries fewer
parameters than a comparable pretrained transformer (Table 5).
Replacing LayerNorm with BatchNorm in the deployed student changes inference latency and peak memory in a device- and
resolution-dependent way, measured across ten mobile devices and
one x86 CPU at three input resolutions (Table 3, Figure 4). The KD
loss weight used throughout sits near the peak of a wide sweep
on CUB (Table 9). Extending the latency measurement to a wider
range of runtimes and accelerators is left open.

=== PAPER: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in Fine-Grained Image Recognition ===

Date of publication xxxx 00, 0000, date of current version xxxx 00, 0000.
Digital Object Identifier 10.1109/ACCESS.2026.0000000

A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between
Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in
Fine-Grained Image Recognition
EDWIN ARKEL RIOS1 , AUGUSTO CHRISTIAN SURYA2 , OSWIN GOSAL2 , FERNANDO MIKAEL2 ,
MARY MADELINE NICOLE2 , KISOON JANG2 , BO-CHENG LAI1 , AND MIN-CHUN HU2
1
2

National Yang Ming Chiao Tung University, Hsinchu, Taiwan
National Tsing Hua University, Hsinchu, Taiwan

ABSTRACT Prior work on fine-grained image recognition (FGIR) has established the importance of
the backbone selection, but has neglected the accuracy-vs-cost trade-offs under different training and
evaluation settings. In this work we conduct a large-scale study with 2,229 experiments across 6 training
and evaluation settings (TrEvS), 16 pretrained backbones, and 17 datasets (9 backbones evaluated on all 17
datasets, with 7 additional backbones evaluated on 4 representative datasets). Preliminary observations on
the effectiveness of data augmentation for fine-grained training motivate us to extend counterfactual attention
learning (CAL), a state-of-the-art method based on data-aware cropping and masking augmentations, with
cross-image discriminative region mixing augmentation (CALMix). Although the counterfactual objective
itself adds no inference cost, CAL—like many FGIR methods—is deployed with a second forward pass
on discriminative crops at evaluation time. Evaluating the same trained models without this second pass,
we find that data-aware augmentation during training alone retains most of the accuracy at a small but
systematic cost of 0.64 points, while significantly reducing inference cost, indicating that the benefit
of such methods is driven by the data-aware training recipe rather than by inference-time cropping. To
support future research we share our code and 825 pretrained checkpoints, loadable as a drop-in library,
at: https://github.com/arkel23/FGIR-Backbones
INDEX TERMS Backbone architectures, benchmark, data augmentation, fine-grained image recognition,
representation learning, transfer learning.
I. INTRODUCTION

Fine-grained image recognition (FGIR) tasks—such as distinguishing bird species or car models—require identifying
subtle visual differences between closely related categories.
While previous works on FGIR focused on modules for
discriminative feature selection [1]–[4], recent studies [5]
highlight that the choice of backbone architecture is a critical
decision in FGIR; the backbone not only dictates downstream
accuracy, but is a major factor in training and deployment
costs [5] as our experiments show in Fig. 3.
However, performance is also influenced by the choice of
training and evaluation settings (TrEvS). Prior work [5]–[8]
primarily benchmarks backbones under generic recognition
settings, overlooking the impact of FGIR-specific strategies.
Therefore, in this work, we systematically study the interactions of different TrEvS—including both generic, frozen or
fine-tuned settings and FGIR-specific configurations—on 16
VOLUME 11, 2023

pretrained backbones spanning CNNs and Transformers—
9 evaluated on all 17 fine-grained datasets and 7 additional
backbones on 4 representative datasets, for 2,229 experiments in total—across datasets with diverse domains and data
densities. Our results show that FGIR-specific TrEvS can
improve accuracy by up to 78% relative to a frozen backbone
(CALMix), but can increase training time to roughly 17×
the frozen baseline and reduce inference throughput by up
to 86%. Based on this observation we take counterfactual
attention learning (CAL) [4], a state-of-the-art data-aware
training method, as a case study. We extend its training-time
augmentation with cross-image discriminative region mixing
(CALMix), and we disentangle the contribution of training
from that of the two-pass, attention-cropped inference its implementation shares with many FGIR methods by evaluating
the same trained models without inference-time crops (CALNC and CALMix-NC). The no-crop evaluations show that the
1

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

accuracy benefit stems primarily from data-aware training, so
deployment cost can be reduced substantially without giving
it up.
Our contribution includes:
• We conduct a systematic study of 2,229 experiments—covering 6 training and evaluation settings, 16
backbones (9 evaluated on all 17 datasets, 7 additional
on 4 representative datasets), and 17 datasets—on the
interaction between backbone and training and evaluation settings. Our results provide practical guidelines for
FGIR system design, highlighting that increasing setting
complexity can improve accuracy by up to 78% in relative terms, while increasing training time to roughly 17×
the frozen baseline and reducing inference throughput
by up to 86%.
• We complement the accuracy–cost analysis with a representational analysis based on centered kernel alignment (CKA) between network stages, showing that the
degree of inter-stage feature differentiation strongly predicts fine-grained accuracy and reflects, at the representation level, the effect of dataset difficulty (RIC) and
backbone choice.
• Using CAL as a case study, we extend its training with cross-image discriminative region mixing
(CALMix), a composition of CAL with existing mixing augmentations, and disentangle training-time benefits from inference-time attention cropping by evaluating the same trained models without crops (CALNC, CALMix-NC). These evaluations show that FGIRspecific gains are driven primarily by data-aware training rather than by two-pass inference.
• We release the training, evaluation, and benchmarking
code together with 825 pretrained checkpoints—
one per dataset, backbone, training strategy
and input resolution—packaged as a dropin library, so loading any of them is a single
call:
create_backbone(
’resnet101’,
dataset=’cub’, strategy=’cal’).

scriptors and global semantics; Wei et al. [15] survey the field.
Most of these methods center on locating and re-processing
discriminative regions, and the multi-stage pipelines that do
so at inference significantly increase deployment cost—the
cost our study quantifies.
B. BACKBONE AND BENCHMARK STUDIES

Recent works have explored the impact of backbone architectures on transfer learning and FGIR. Kornblith et al. [6]
analyzed whether higher ImageNet accuracy leads to better
downstream performance, while Goldblum et al. [7] and Jeevan et al. [8] focused on the impact of backbone in general image classification. Their works demonstrated that the
backbone can make as much of a difference as a specialized
discriminative part selection method. Ye et al. [5] investigated
backbones for weakly supervised FGIR and observed that
CNN backbones tend to perform better on structured tasks
such as aircraft and car classification. However, the relative
performance between CNNs and Vision Transformers can
vary depending on training configurations and optimization
choices. These studies benchmark under generic settings;
none of them measures how FGIR-specific training strategies
shift the accuracy–cost trade-off, which is the gap this work
addresses.
C. DATA AUGMENTATION AND REPRESENTATION
ANALYSIS

II. RELATED WORKS
A. FINE-GRAINED IMAGE RECOGNITION (FGIR)

Cross-image mixing augmentations combine regions of two
training images: CutMix [16] pastes random crops across
images, while SnapMix [17], intra-class part swapping [18],
and S3Mix [19] make the mixing semantically aware, which
matters in fine-grained settings where a random patch can
destroy the discriminative cue. CAL [4] instead uses its attention maps for data-aware cropping and masking; CALMix
combines the two lines by mixing attention-selected discriminative regions within a class. To analyze what the trained
networks learn, we use centered kernel alignment [20], a similarity index between layer representations previously used to
compare vision architectures, which to our knowledge has not
been applied at this scale to FGIR.

Early FGIR methods relied on part annotations or bounding
boxes to guide localization [1]. While effective, these methods are difficult to scale due to the cost of annotations. Later
works leveraged image-level labels with attention mechanisms, such as MA-CNN [2], which learned part-aware features without extra supervision, and WS-DAN [3], which uses
its attention maps both to augment training data and to crop
discriminative regions at inference; CAL [4] adds counterfactual supervision on top of this pipeline. Transformer-based
methods select discriminative tokens or regions in a similar
spirit [9]–[11]. Recent methods continue this line: CMALNet [12] treats network layers as mutually teaching experts
whose attention regions serve as augmentation, HERBS [13]
refines multi-scale features while suppressing background,
and CSQA-Net [14] models interactions between part de-

Our experimental protocol consists of two stages. In the
first stage, we perform a learning-rate search per backbone,
dataset, and setting—over {0.3, 0.1, 0.03, 0.01, 0.003} for
the frozen setting and {0.03, 0.01, 0.003, 0.001} for the finetuned settings—selecting the best learning rate based on performance on a held-out evaluation subset of the training data,
and use it for all subsequent experiments. Each backbone
therefore competes at its own best learning rate, so backbone
comparisons are not confounded by a shared training recipe.
In the second stage, we conduct multi-seed experiments using
the selected learning rate for each dataset to account for
stochasticity in deep learning optimization and obtain more
robust performance estimates.

A. DATASETS

We evaluate our approach on 17 fine-grained datasets spanning diverse domains and scales, as summarized in Table 1.
We first conduct controlled experiments on 4 representative
datasets. We then extend our evaluation to a broader set of
13 additional datasets for comprehensive analysis. Example
images of each dataset are shown in Figure 1.
B. MODELS

Our experiments consider 16 backbone architectures spanning both CNNs and Transformers, summarized in Table 2.
We begin with a core set of 9 backbones evaluated on all
17 datasets. To cover a wider range of architectures, pretraining schemes, and model scales, we then add 7 additional
backbones for extended experiments on the 4 representative
datasets. Unless otherwise noted, analyses spanning all 17
datasets use the 9 core backbones, while analyses restricted
to the 4 representative datasets use all 16.
C. TRAINING & EVALUATION SETTINGS

Prior work [5], [6], [8] typically evaluates backbones under
fixed configurations, namely Frozen (FZ), where only the
classifier head is trained while the backbone remains fixed,
and Fine-Tuned (FT), where all backbone parameters are
updated on the target dataset. However, they neglected FGIRspecific training strategies that can affect performance. To adVOLUME 11, 2023

dress this, we incorporate Counterfactual Attention Learning (CAL) [4], which improves attention by leveraging bilinear attention pooling (BAP) and counterfactual supervision to
compare factual and counterfactual predictions, encouraging
the model to focus on more discriminative regions.
D. DISENTANGLING DATA-AWARE TRAINING FROM
INFERENCE-TIME CROPPING

The counterfactual objective of CAL adds no computation
at inference [4]; its deployment cost instead comes from the
two-stage inference pipeline its implementation inherits from
attention-cropping methods [3], and which many FGIR methods share [1], [2], [9], [11]: an attention-like module—BAP in
the case of CAL—first predicts discriminative regions from
a global forward pass, and the image is then cropped for a
second local forward pass. To separate the impact of these
inference attention-crops from the data-aware training, we
evaluate the CAL-trained models with the attention cropping
at inference removed. We refer to this variant as CAL-NC
(CAL No Crops); it is an evaluation choice rather than a new
method, reusing the trained weights unchanged. As shown
in Section IV-E, CAL-NC reduces inference cost at a small
but statistically significant accuracy cost, which we quantify
there. These results suggest that the primary benefit of CAL
comes from its data-aware training mechanism. Building on
this observation, we explore whether stronger data-aware
3

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

augmentation can further improve performance. Specifically,
in addition to attention cropping and masking, we incorporate cross-image mixing [16]–[19] by swapping discriminative regions of two images from the same class; we denote
this extended training strategy as CALMix. CALMix is a
composition of CAL’s attention-guided augmentations with
existing cross-image mixing strategies, and its justification
is empirical: it improves robustness by encouraging regionlevel feature learning. Its no-crop evaluation counterpart is
CALMix-NC.
E. EVALUATION METRICS

We evaluate performance and cost using the following metrics:
• Top-1 Accuracy. The percentage of samples where the
predicted class matches the ground-truth label.
• Training Time. The total time required to complete a
full training run, measured in minutes.
• Batched Test Throughput. The number of samples
processed per second during inference, computed under batched evaluation, as opposed to single-sample.
Throughput is measured for the frozen, fine-tuned and
CAL settings; because CALMix alters training-time
augmentation only and leaves the inference graph identical to CAL’s, its throughput is taken from the corresponding CAL measurement rather than measured separately.
• Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) [20]. CKA measures representational similarity between network layers
and is defined as
vec(X T X ) · vec(Y T Y )
.
CKAlinear (X , Y ) =
∥X T X ∥F ∥Y T Y ∥F
We use linear CKA to analyze the learned representations of the trained backbones (Section IV-G).
To enable fair comparison across heterogeneous scales, all
reported values are normalized using min-max scaling before
visualization:
x − min(x)
x′ =
max(x) − min(x)
This allows consistent visualization of the accuracy–efficiency
trade-off within a unified plot.

IV. RESULTS

Before presenting the analyses, we first summarize the evaluation setup and aggregation strategy. The Dataset groups,
Backbone, Image Size, and Training & Evaluation Setting
analyses (Sections IV-A, IV-B, IV-D and IV-E) aim to study
the interaction between model design choices and the performance–cost trade-off, where higher performance generally comes with increased computational cost. The Backbone
analysis is conducted across all 17 datasets, while the Image
Size and Setting analyses are evaluated on the 4 original
datasets. Across the three later analyses, we report min-max
normalized values of accuracy, training time, and inference
throughput to ensure fair comparison across heterogeneous
scales.
A. EFFECT OF BACKBONE ON DIFFERENT DATASET

To analyze the effect of backbone choice across datasets, we
focus on the CAL-NC setting, which provides a favorable
accuracy–cost trade-off. We characterize dataset difficulty
with the Ratio of Images to Classes (RIC), defined as RIC =
Ntrain /C 2 , where Ntrain is the number of training images and
C the number of classes. RIC is close to 1 for ImageNet (1.28)
and decreases for datasets with fewer images, fewer images
per class, or more classes, so a lower RIC indicates a harder
transfer regime. The squared denominator is what makes
RIC a good difficulty descriptor: across the 17 datasets, perdataset mean accuracy rank-correlates with RIC at Spearman
ρ = 0.68–0.79 depending on the setting, compared with
ρ = 0.52–0.60 for images per class (Ntrain /C), while class
count and training-set size alone are weak or non-significant.
Writing RIC = (Ntrain /C) · (1/C) shows why: difficulty
compounds when fewer images per class meet finer class
granularity. The datasets are grouped by RIC order of
magnitude, ranging from -3 (hardest) to 1. RIC establishes
VOLUME 11, 2023

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

FIGURE 3. Comparison of the 9 core backbones at image size 224. Accuracy as Performance, Train Time & Inference Throughput as Cost; for each
backbone, each metric is first averaged over datasets within a setting and min-max normalized, and each box aggregates the backbone’s per-setting
values. Throughput is grouped under Cost but higher is better. Costs measured on a single RTX 3090 GPU.

FIGURE 4. Average relative differences of Accuracy & Cost between Image Size 224 vs 384, per training and evaluation setting. Image Size 224 as Baseline.

percentage axis; the exact accuracy differences are given in the text.

FIGURE 5. Comparison of FZ, FT, CAL and CAL-NC settings with Image Size 224 (IS224) vs. Image Size 384 (IS384) across 16 Backbones and 4 Datasets.
Accuracy as Performance, Train Time & Inference Throughput as Cost; each metric is min-max normalized and each box aggregates all backbone–dataset
pairs of one setting. Throughput is grouped under Cost but higher is better. Costs measured on a single RTX 3090 GPU.

a meaningful difficulty grouping: accuracy increases monotonically with the RIC group from -3 through 0, whether
measured over all 9 core backbones (29% to 92% mean) or
over the best backbone per group as plotted in Figure 2; the
VOLUME 11, 2023

single group-1 dataset (Food-101) performs comparably to
group 0.
Within each difficulty regime, the performance of the backbones also varies. Swin-B leads in the most challenging
5

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

regimes (RIC groups -3 and -2): by roughly 11 points over
the next-best backbone on the single group -3 dataset (SoyGlobal), and by roughly 3 points averaged over the three
group -2 datasets, while ConvNeXt-B leads in the moderate
regime (RIC -1). In the easiest regimes (0 and 1) the top backbones lie within half a point of each other (VAN B-3 narrowly
best at 0; Swin-B and ConvNeXt-B effectively tied at 1),
so backbone choice matters most as dataset difficulty increases. The hierarchical window-based attention mechanism
of Swin-B appears better suited for capturing subtle interclass variations in highly challenging fine-grained scenarios,
whereas on datasets with more separable discriminative cues
most modern backbones perform comparably.
Overall, these findings demonstrate that architectural robustness in FGIR is dataset-dependent rather than universally consistent across benchmarks. As a result, dataset difficulty should be considered during FGIR system design,
since different backbone families may show different behavior under varying fine-grained complexity regimes. RIC provides a practical analytical tool for characterizing dataset
difficulty and guiding backbone selection prior to extensive
experimentation.
B. PERFORMANCE & COST OF DIFFERENT BACKBONES

We next analyze the relationship between model complexity
and computational efficiency across backbone architectures.
Table 2 reports the number of parameters and computational
cost (GMACs) for each model, while Fig. 3 visualizes their
training and inference efficiency.
As shown in Table 2 and Figure 3, more modern and
complex backbone architectures generally incur higher
computational cost, resulting in longer training time and
lower inference throughput. Classical CNN-based models
such as ResNet-101 exhibit lower computational cost and
faster training and inference compared to Transformer-based
models such as ViT-B/16, which are more computationally
demanding, though not every classical CNN does: VGG19 (19.65 GMACs) exceeds ViT B-16 (17.57). While more
complex architectures often achieve stronger performance,
the relationship between complexity and accuracy is not
strictly monotonic, and some highly complex models fail
to provide gains that justify their additional cost, such as
ResNet-101x3 (BiT-M). Among all evaluated backbones,
Swin-B and ConvNeXt-B achieve the best accuracy–cost
trade-off (Figure 3, Table 3), generally outperforming both
lighter CNNs and heavier Transformer variants while maintaining moderate computational overhead, though not on every dataset: in Table 3, DeiT3-L leads SoyGene and Swin-L
leads SoyLocal.
C. EFFECT OF PRETRAINING AND MODEL SCALING

Beyond architectural differences, we further analyze the impact of pretraining data scale and model size on FGIR performance using Table 3. Based on ConvNeXt and Swin models,
increasing pretraining scale from IN1k to IN21k consistently improves accuracy, particularly on more challenging
6

datasets, where the largest gains are 7 points on SoyGene
(Swin-B) and 21 points on SoyLocal (ConvNeXt-B). The
trend does not extend to every family: DeiT3 B-16 drops from
72.31 to 54.10 on SoyGene when its pretraining switches
from IN1k to IN21k. In contrast, increasing model capacity from Base to Large yields marginal and inconsistent
changes for ConvNeXt and Swin, within 1.5 points either
way and not uniformly positive. DeiT3 is the exception:
scaling from Base to Large gains 1.5–2.3 points on three
datasets and 19.5 points on SoyGene, where the Base variant
is anomalously weak (54.10). Capacity therefore helps when
the smaller variant fails to fit the domain at all, but not
otherwise. Overall, these results indicate that pretraining
scale is a more influential factor than model size in FGIR
performance, highlighting the importance of data-centric
rather than parameter-centric scaling strategies.
D. PERFORMANCE & COST OF DIFFERENT IMAGE SIZES

As illustrated in Figure 4, increasing the image resolution from 224 to 384 generally improves accuracy but increases computational cost significantly. This comparison
covers the four settings evaluated at both resolutions (FZ,
FT, CAL, and CAL-NC); CALMix was trained only at 224
on the representative datasets, so it is outside the resolution
sweep. For the three Swin variants, the 3842 runs load the
corresponding window-12, 3842 -pretrained checkpoints, so
window size and pretraining resolution change together with
the input size; all other backbones reuse the same pretrained
weights at both resolutions. Shown in Figure 5, on average,
higher resolution increases training time by up to 143% while
reducing inference throughput by up to 65%, yet yields only
modest accuracy improvements. In extreme cases, training
time increases can exceed 200%. These results indicate that
accuracy gains from higher resolution are not proportional
to the additional computational cost, suggesting that aggressive resolution scaling is unlikely to be a computationally
efficient strategy for improving FGIR systems.
E. PERFORMANCE & COST OF DIFFERENT SETTINGS

As illustrated in Figures 7 and 6, increasing training and
evaluation complexity generally improves FGIR accuracy,
but at significant computational cost. Using the Frozen (FZ)
setting as the baseline, the largest gains come from CALMix:
averaged over the 4 representative datasets and all 16 backbones, its relative accuracy increase is 78%, at the cost of a
1632% relative increase in training time (roughly 17× the
FZ training time). Furthermore, cropping during inference
reduces efficiency, with CAL and CALMix decreasing inference throughput by up to 86% relative to FZ.
To address the inference issue, we evaluate the same
trained models without inference-time cropping (CAL-NC
and CALMix-NC). These variants restore inference efficiency to levels comparable with FZ while retaining most
of the accuracy gain: a mean relative increase of 76% for
CALMix-NC and 60% for CAL-NC. The crops are not free,
and we quantify what removing them costs rather than asVOLUME 11, 2023

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

Throughput as Cost; each metric is min-max normalized and each box aggregates all backbone–dataset pairs of one setting. Throughput is grouped under
Cost but higher is better; the CALMix and CALMix-NC throughputs are taken from the corresponding CAL measurements (identical inference graph). Costs
measured on a single RTX 3090 GPU.

FIGURE 7. Average relative differences of Accuracy & Cost between FZ Setting and the 5 other Training and Evaluation Settings. FZ Setting as Baseline.

serting it is negligible. Paired over the 153 dataset–backbone
cells at 2242 , dropping the crops lowers accuracy in 133
of them, by a mean of 0.64 points and a median of 0.36
(Wilcoxon p = 7 × 10−17 ); the change stays within one point
in 71% of cells, and the largest losses are 6.94 (Cotton, BEiT
B-16) and 6.56 (SoyGene, ResNet-101). For CALMix the
corresponding loss is 0.43 points over 64 cells (p = 2×10−7 ).
The reduction is therefore small and systematic rather than
absent: it buys back the throughput of a single forward pass
at a consistent but bounded accuracy price, which is the tradeoff we recommend, not a free one.
The 448 side sweep isolates CALMix’s own contribution,
because there each backbone was trained both ways on the
same datasets—four CNNs on all three, and ViT B-16 on
CUB alone: adding cross-image mixing to CAL gains 0.34
points with inference crops and 0.46 without, a 4.5% and
6.1% reduction in relative error over the 13 backbone–dataset
pairs (Table 7). The gain is consistent in sign but small, which
is why we present CALMix as a refinement of data-aware
training rather than as the paper’s main result.
VOLUME 11, 2023

Overall, while FGIR-specific training strategies consistently improve accuracy, they also introduce substantial computational overhead. CAL-NC and CALMix-NC mitigate the
inference inefficiency of their original counterparts while
maintaining comparable accuracy. As a result, CAL-NC and
CALMix-NC provide a strong accuracy–efficiency tradeoff, making them practical choices for real-world FGIR applications.
For context against specialized FGIR methods, published
results reach 93.1 on CUB (HERBS [13]) and 94.7 on FGVCAircraft (CMAL-Net [12]), typically at higher input resolutions; CAL itself reports 94.2 on Aircraft with inferencetime cropping [4]. Our best no-crop configurations reach 91.5
(CUB) and 92.8 (Aircraft) at 2242 (Table 3), and 92.4 (CUB,
ConvNeXt-L) and 94.8 (Aircraft, ConvNeXt-B) at 3842 (Table 6)—on Aircraft comparable to both published crop-based
results, and on CUB within 0.7 of HERBS, without the second
forward pass.

7

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

F. SENSITIVITY TO THE LEARNING RATE

The learning-rate search that precedes the main sweep also
measures how fragile each setting is: for every dataset–
backbone–setting cell we take the coefficient of variation
of validation accuracy across the learning rates tried, so a
large value marks a configuration that only works when the
learning rate is right. Because the frozen setting was swept
over a different range, we compare on the 4 learning rates the
fine-tuned settings share, and on the 3 that also include FZ.
The data-aware training recipe makes training substantially less sensitive to the learning rate: over the 15 datasets
searched (all but the two largest, DAFB and iNaturalist), the
mean coefficient of variation falls from 28.0 (FT) to 18.6
(CAL), with CAL the more stable of the two in 122 of 163
dataset–backbone cells (Wilcoxon p = 4 × 10−12 ), and to
14.2 for CALMix against CAL’s 19.2 on the 3 datasets where
both were searched. FZ is not part of this ordering—on the
grid it shares with the others it is indistinguishable from FT
(p = 0.27)—so the effect follows from the CAL recipe rather
than from optimizing fewer parameters. Which component of
that recipe—the attention-guided augmentation, the counterfactual objective, or bilinear attention pooling—is responsible
cannot be separated by the settings evaluated here.
Fragility tracks the same axes as accuracy. Across the 9
core backbones it is strongly anti-correlated with accuracy
(ρ = −0.92, p = 0.0005), ranging from 11.4 for BiT-M
ResNet-101x3 to 44.5 for ResNet-101; ImageNet-21k pretraining lowers it for both backbones available under both
pretraining schemes (Swin-B 21.6 against 27.6, lower in 8 of
8 cells, p = 0.008; ConvNeXt-B 23.5 against 38.0, lower
in 7 of 8, p = 0.055, with SoyLocal under CAL moving
the other way); and across datasets it grows as RIC falls
(ρ = −0.78, p = 0.0006). The accuracy spread a change
of learning rate induces is roughly 5 times the spread across
seeds (FT: 6.9 against 1.3 points, Table 5). The backbones
and datasets this study finds hardest are also those whose
results depend most on tuning, which adds a robustness
argument to the accuracy and cost case for the data-aware
settings.
G. A REPRESENTATIONAL VIEW: INTER-STAGE CKA

The analyses above characterize what accuracy and cost result from each design choice, but not why some backbones,
datasets, and settings succeed while others fail. To address
this, we analyze the internal representations of the trained
models using linear centered kernel alignment (CKA). We
study the 9 core backbones under the FZ, FT, and CAL
settings—all 17 datasets at 2242 and the 4 representative
datasets at 3842 , so 9 × 17 × 3 + 9 × 4 × 3 = 567 runs
from the serial-21 checkpoints. The no-crop variants reuse the
CAL-trained checkpoints and differ only at inference, so their
training-split representations coincide with CAL’s; CALMix
checkpoints exist only for the 4 representative datasets and
are omitted. For each run, we extract unpooled features at
the output of the four network stages—the stage outputs of
hierarchical models (BiT-M ResNet-101x3 uses the same four
8

residual stages as the other ResNets, and VGG-19 the outputs
of its four deepest convolutional blocks), or four equally
spaced blocks for isotropic Transformers (blocks 3, 6, 9, and
12 of 12)—over the entire training split at batch size 8, and
compute the linear CKA between every pair of stages. We
summarize each run by the average CKA of the first stage
against the deeper stages (stage-1 CKA); a low value means
the network produces strongly differentiated representations
across its depth, whereas a high value means the stages are
largely redundant.
Inter-stage feature differentiation predicts accuracy. As
shown in Fig. 8, stage-1 CKA is strongly negatively correlated
with Top-1 accuracy: pooled over all 567 runs the correlation
is Pearson r = −0.75 (Spearman ρ = −0.69, p < 10−80 ),
and it is in fact slightly tighter within each individual setting
(r = −0.76 to −0.79), confirming it is an intra-setting effect
rather than an artifact of comparing settings. Reported pvalues are uncorrected; the main effect (p < 10−80 ) remains
significant under Bonferroni or Benjamini–Hochberg correction across the full test battery, while individual per-setting
and per-metric cells are treated as descriptive. Models that
transform their representation across depth—building progressively more specialized features—achieve higher finegrained accuracy, whereas models whose stages remain mutually redundant perform poorly, independently of parameter
count or computational cost.
The predictive signal is concentrated in early stages.
Comparing depth summaries (Fig. 9), the stage-1-vs-deeper
summary is the strongest predictor in every setting (ρ up
to −0.73), the mean inter-stage summary is intermediate
(ρ = −0.63 pooled), and the last-stage-vs-rest summary
is the weakest (ρ = −0.53). Resolving the CKA matrices
into individual stage pairs confirms the same ordering—
the earliest pairs carry the signal while the deepest pair is
uninformative—so the simple stage-1 summary is the recommended single-number descriptor. This indicates that finegrained recognition depends on how strongly a backbone reshapes its early-stage features, contrasting with the common
transfer-learning view that early layers are generic and only
late layers need adaptation.
A representational view of dataset difficulty. The RIC
grouping introduced in Section IV-A orders datasets by difficulty; at the dataset level, inter-stage CKA separates the two
ends of that ordering, distinguishing the ultra-fine-grained
leaf datasets from the natural-image ones. CKA decreases
with the RIC order of magnitude (Fig. 10; Spearman ρ =
−0.50, p = 0.043) and increases as accuracy falls (Fig. 11;
ρ = −0.52, p = 0.033); these dataset-level correlations
are modest and driven by the two clusters visible in the
figures, in contrast to the run-level effect above. The ultrafine-grained leaf datasets (SoyLocal, SoyGlobal, SoyGene,
SoyAgeing, Cotton) sit at high inter-stage CKA (∼ 0.7):
on these domains the ImageNet-pretrained backbones build
little differentiated feature hierarchy, the stages remain nearredundant (representational collapse), and accuracy is low.
Natural-image datasets (Dogs, Pets, Food, Flowers, CUB,

Cars) differentiate strongly (CKA ∼ 0.35–0.45) and score
highly. Thus RIC and inter-stage CKA are complementary
descriptors of difficulty—one based on data statistics, the
other on learned representations—and together they suggest
that the hardest FGIR regimes are limited by representation
VOLUME 11, 2023

Per-class difficulty and follow-up directions. Finally, a
per-class analysis is consistent with this picture (Fig. 12). On
CUB only 3.5% of classes (7 of 200) are hard for most backbones, and on FGVC-Aircraft only 2%—difficulty is concentrated in a small set of intrinsically ambiguous classes. On
the ultra-fine-grained datasets, however, 84% (SoyGene) and
91% (SoyLocal) of classes fall below 50% mean accuracy,
and difficulty is largely backbone-independent. This suggests
that for ultra-fine-grained recognition, domain-adapted pretraining and representation-level interventions are likely to be
more effective than backbone substitution, which we identify
as a promising direction for future work. More broadly, the
CKA–accuracy relationships reported here are correlational;
a controlled depth-freezing or stage-wise linear-probing experiment is needed to establish causality, and we leave this
intervention to future work.

9

Rios et al.: A Large-Scale Study on the Interaction between Backbone and Training and Evaluation Setting in FGIR

image ones. Overall, these results highlight the effectiveness
of FGIR-specific, data-aware training and demonstrate that
carefully designed efficient variants can achieve a strong
balance between accuracy and computational cost, providing
practical guidance for future cost-aware FGIR system design.

V. CONCLUSION

Overall, our results show that stronger performance is generally accompanied by higher computational cost. Across
backbones, we observe a non-uniform accuracy–efficiency
relationship, with modern architectures such as Swin and
ConvNeXt achieving the best trade-offs. Similarly, increasing input resolution generally improves accuracy but incurs
higher computational cost, and under the frozen setting the
mean gain is negative over the 9 core backbones, driven by
a collapse of BEiT B-16 at 3842 ; naive resolution scaling is
therefore not an efficient strategy. For training and evaluation
settings, more complex configurations consistently improve
accuracy by up to 78% in relative terms (CALMix), while
increasing training time to roughly 17× the frozen baseline
and reducing inference throughput by up to 86%. In particular, CAL improves accuracy significantly over FT but
introduces higher computational cost in both training and
inference. Building on this, CALMix further enhances performance, while evaluating the same trained models without
inference-time cropping (CAL-NC and CALMix-NC) costs
0.64 points on average across 153 dataset–backbone cells
while restoring near-frozen throughput, indicating that most
of the gain comes from the training recipe rather than from
two-pass inference. These settings also differ in how much
tuning they demand: data-aware augmentation reduces the
accuracy spread across the learning-rate grid by about a
third relative to plain fine-tuning, and the backbones and
datasets that score lowest are also those whose results depend most on that choice. Beyond the accuracy–cost tradeoff, our representational analysis shows that fine-grained accuracy is tightly linked to how strongly a backbone differentiates its features across stages: inter-stage CKA predicts
accuracy (ρ = −0.69 for the early-stage summary) and,
at the dataset level, separates the hardest ultra-fine-grained
datasets—which exhibit representational collapse that backbone substitution alone does not resolve—from the natural10

This section provides visual examples complementing the
CALMix description in the main text. Fig. 13 illustrates
the three data augmentation operations applied for CALMix
training across 3 representative datasets: FGVC-Aircraft [21],
Stanford Cars [24], and CUB-200-2011 [22].
Each figure shows four rows: the original sample images
(Samples), cropping (Crop), masking (Mask), and mixup
(Mix). The Crop and Mask operations are inherited from CAL
[4], while the Mix operation is the additional augmentation
introduced by CALMix, which swaps discriminative regions
between images of the same class to encourage region-level
feature learning.
Across all three domains, the Mix operation exposes the
model to a broader distribution of discriminative region combinations within the same class. The measured effect is a
small mean gain rather than a uniform one: CALMix is below
CAL in 4 of the 13 backbone–dataset pairs with inference
crops (Table 7), and the mean gain is smaller than the seed-toseed standard deviation of CALMix itself (Table 5). However,
this richer augmentation pipeline increases the number of
training samples processed per epoch, which accounts for the
longer training times observed for CALMix relative to CAL
in the settings analysis.
APPENDIX B BACKBONE AND SETTING SELECTION
GUIDE

To assist practitioners in applying our findings, Fig. 14 provides a decision flowchart for how to select a backbone and
training & evaluation settings given a new FGIR dataset. The
selection process first considers the primary computational
constraint: (1) maximizing accuracy, (2) minimizing training
cost, or (3) minimizing inference cost. It then considers the
dataset difficulty, characterized by the order of magnitude
(OOM) of the Ratio of Images to Classes (RIC) introduced
in the main text.
The backbone recommendations within each branch follow
the RIC analysis: Swin-B [39] leads in the most challenging
regimes (RIC orders of magnitude −3 and −2), by roughly 11
points on the single group −3 dataset and 3 points averaged
over the three group −2 datasets, while ConvNeXt-B [40]
leads in the moderate regime (RIC −1). In the easiest regimes
(0 and 1) the top backbones lie within half a point of each
other, so backbone choice matters most as dataset difficulty
increases; this ordering is also reflected in the CKA analysis,
where Swin-B’s hierarchical window attention differentiates
its representations more strongly on the hardest datasets. The
setting recommendations follow from the settings analysis:
when training cost is constrained, a frozen setup avoids the
roughly 17× training-time factor of the most complex setting.

APPENDIX E RESULTS AT IMAGE SIZE 384

Table 6 reports every backbone at 3842 on the 4 representative
