VOLUME 11, 2023

=== PAPER: Cultivar Identification from Leaf Images with a Semantic Adaptation Warmup for Frozen Vision Transformers ===

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

1

Cultivar Identification from Leaf Images with a
Semantic Adaptation Warmup for Frozen Vision Transformers
Edwin Arkel Rios Student Member, IEEE, Femiloye Oyerinde, Fernando Mikael, Oswin Gosal, Min-Chun
Hu, Senior Member, IEEE, and Bo-Cheng Lai

Abstract—Identifying the cultivar of a crop from its leaves
underpins breeding programs and seed certification, but it is
done by hand: the visual differences between cultivars are
finer than those between species, and labeled data is scarce.
The usual remedy, adapting a network pretrained on a generic
corpus, transfers poorly: across thirteen configurations built on
a frozen Vision Transformer, accuracy falls from between 82.9%
and 88.7% on a species-level benchmark to at most 69.7% on
the easiest of ten cultivar datasets, and a linear classifier on
the frozen representation reaches only 17.9% on SoyGlobal.
We attribute this to a semantic gap between what generic
pretraining encodes and what separating cultivars requires, and
we close it with a Semantic Adaptation Warmup (SAW): the
inserted adaptation modules are first trained against a supervised
contrastive objective on a pooled corpus of cultivar imagery, and
only then is the classifier trained. The warmup is driven by
a Dual-Attention-driven Mix Augmentation SupCon (DAMAS)
strategy, which locates discriminative regions from the attention
of two early transformer layers rather than a full rollout,
reducing the augmentation cost from the O(L · N 2 ) of a [CLS]row rollout to O(N 2 ). Warmup happens once and adds nothing
to inference cost. Across ten cultivar datasets at 448 × 448 the
warmup raises average top-1 accuracy by 5.9 points over the
same architecture without it, exceeds the strongest competing
parameter-efficient method by 12.6 points and the strongest fully
fine-tuned method in our comparison by 5.1 points, while training
0.51 M parameters per 200-class task, at lower FLOPs and higher
throughput than the alternatives.
Index Terms—Cultivar identification, plant phenotyping, precision agriculture, ultra-fine-grained image recognition, parameterefficient transfer learning, vision transformer, contrastive learning

I. I NTRODUCTION
This article is an extended version of work presented at the Efficient Deep
Learning for Foundation Models workshop at ECCV 2024 [1]. The inter-layer
adapter and its evaluation appeared there; the semantic adaptation warmup, the
gap measurement that motivates it, and all warmup experiments and ablations
are new.
This work was supported by the National Science and Technology Council,
Taiwan, under grants NSTC 111-2221-E-A49-092-MY3, NSTC 113-2640-EA49-005, NSTC 112-2221-E-007-079-MY3 and NSTC 113-2218-E-007-020.
We also thank the National Center for High-performance Computing (NCHC)
and NYCU HPC for providing computational and storage resources.
Edwin Arkel Rios and Bo-Cheng Lai are with the Institute of Electronics, National Yang Ming Chiao Tung University, Hsinchu, Taiwan (email:
is with Cohere Labs Community. Fernando Mikael, Oswin Gosal, and
Min-Chun Hu are with the Computer Science Department, National Tsing
(Edwin Arkel Rios and Femiloye Oyerinde contributed equally to this work.)
(Corresponding author: Edwin Arkel Rios.)

T

ELLING one cultivar of a crop from another by eye is
a routine and consequential task in agriculture. Breeding
programmes must track which variety is growing in which
plot, and seed certification depends on confirming varietal
identity; both are done today by trained inspectors working
from leaf morphology. It is slow, and it does not scale: expert
accuracy falls as the number of candidate cultivars grows, and
annotating images to train a model requires the same scarce
expertise, so labeled data stays thin — often only a handful
of images per cultivar. Figure 1 shows what makes the task
hard: leaves of different cultivars look nearly identical, while
leaves of one cultivar vary widely.

Fig. 1. Samples of different classes from the ultra-fine-grained SoyGene
and SoyLocal datasets [2], highlighting subtle inter-class differences and
pronounced intra-class diversity.

Automated visual recognition in agrifood has advanced
quickly on tasks of this shape. Deep networks now classify
potato breeds from tuber and leaf imagery [3], grade defects
in soybean kernels [4], identify individual cattle from muzzle
patterns [5], detect plant disease in real time from a drone [6],
and discriminate visually similar seedlings under varying field
conditions [7]. What these share with cultivar identification is
that the distinction to be made is finer than the species level,
so the discriminative evidence is subtle, distributed, and easily
swamped by variation within a class. This regime is known
as ultra-fine-grained image recognition (UFGIR) [2], and it
separates cultivar identification from conventional fine-grained
recognition, which stops at species-level granularity [8], [9].

A. The problem: pretrained features do not transfer to cultivar
granularity
The standard recipe for a task with few labels is to take
a network pretrained on a large generic corpus and adapt it.
For cultivar recognition that recipe fails at the first step, and
it fails by a wide margin.
We measured this directly. Holding a Vision Transformer’s
[10] pretrained weights frozen and training only lightweight
modules on top, we evaluated thirteen such configurations —
those of Tab. II that were run on both benchmarks — at

0000–0000/00$00.00 © 2021 IEEE

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

224×224 resolution on a species-level fine-grained benchmark
(CUB-200-2011, birds) and ten cultivar-level leaf datasets. On
the species benchmark every configuration reaches between
82.9% and 88.7% top-1 accuracy. On the cultivar datasets the
best of those same thirteen configurations, on the easiest of
the ten datasets, reaches 69.7%, and the weakest combinations
fall below 10%. Every configuration on the species benchmark
outperforms every configuration on every cultivar dataset, with
13.2 points separating the worst of the former from the best of
the latter. A plain linear classifier on the frozen representation
illustrates the same point at its starkest: 17.9% on SoyGlobal
and 19.5% on SoyGene.
The shortfall is visible in the representation itself, before
any adaptation is attempted. We computed the layer-wise
Centered Kernel Alignment [11] of the pretrained encoder on
all eleven datasets under one protocol, with no training of
any kind. Averaged over the deepest third of the encoder,
similarity between layers is 0.56 to 0.57 on the species
benchmark but between 0.709 and 0.743 on every one of the
ten cultivar datasets, at both input resolutions we evaluate: the
two ranges never overlap, on any dataset, at either resolution.
On cultivar imagery the deep layers of the pretrained encoder
have largely stopped distinguishing one layer’s output from
another’s, which is the representational form of the same
failure the accuracies report. A second, independent measure
agrees. At matched class count, the log maximum evidence
[12] of the frozen features is 1.50 on the species benchmark
against 1.23 to 1.26 on every cultivar dataset of comparable
granularity.
This is not a shortage of data. SoyGene carries more training
images than the species benchmark and still scores far lower.
It is a mismatch between what generic pretraining encodes and
what separating cultivars requires — a semantic gap between
the pretraining domain and the target task. Closing it, rather
than merely adding capacity on top of it, is what this article
addresses.
The gap also explains why parameter-efficient adaptation
is the natural setting here rather than an afterthought. Fully
fine-tuning a transformer for every cultivar task is expensive to store and to serve, and deployment in this domain
means a handheld or airborne device rather than a datacenter.
Parameter-efficient transfer learning (PETL) methods [13]–
[15] answer that by freezing the backbone and training small
inserted modules, so one copy of the backbone serves many
tasks. But a frozen backbone is exactly the case where the gap
bites hardest, because the representation cannot move to meet
the task. Two symptoms follow. The deeper encoder layers
converge toward nearly identical representations, a degradation
of representational diversity known as attention collapse [16]–
[19] and visible in the layer-wise similarity of Fig. 4. And
the attention that remains settles on background rather than
on the leaf, as Fig. 2 shows — a shortcut [20] that a model
with few labeled images is readily driven to. A method that
adapts a frozen backbone for cultivar recognition therefore has
to address the gap explicitly.

2

Fig. 2. Samples and attention maps on SoyAgeing [2] for different PETL
methods: linear classifier ViT [10], Adapter [21], ILA (Ours) and SAW (Ours).
Previous baselines display higher attention to background regions.

B. Approach
We introduce a Semantic Adaptation Warmup (SAW): a
stage that trains the inserted adaptation modules against a
supervised contrastive objective, on a pooled corpus of cultivar
imagery, before the classifier is trained. Its augmentation is
data-aware — discriminative regions are located from the
attention of two early transformer layers and swapped between
images, which costs O(N 2 ) rather than the O(L · N 2 ) of
a full attention rollout [22]–[24], where L is the number
of transformer layers and N the sequence length. We call
this augmentation Dual-Attention-driven Mix Augmentation
SupCon (DAMAS). Earlier work has added self-supervised or
contrastive losses to fine-tuning [25]–[28], but trains them on
the target dataset alone and augments without regard to where
the discriminative evidence lies. The warmup adds nothing
to inference cost: it changes only the state the modules start
from. Nor is it tied to the module it initializes: applied to a
plain adapter, with no inter-layer module at all, it is worth 8.8
points across the ten datasets.
Across ten cultivar datasets, the warmup raises average
top-1 accuracy by 5.9 points at 448 × 448 over the same
architecture trained without it, and by 6.7 points at 224 × 224;
against the strongest competing parameter-efficient method,
FFVT, the margins are 12.6 and 11.1 points respectively. It
trains 0.51 M parameters per task, of which the 200-class head
is 0.15 M — the head scales with the label set. Inference costs
67.1 GFLOPs against 78.5 for the frozen baseline, with higher
batched throughput than every parameter-efficient method we
compare against.

C. Relation to our earlier work
This article extends a paper published at the Efficient Deep
Learning for Foundation Models workshop at ECCV 2024 [1].
That paper contributed:
1) an Inter-Layer Adapter (ILA) that spatially downsamples the token grid between frozen encoder blocks,
introducing a hierarchical inductive bias that counters
attention collapse while reducing inference cost; and

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

2) an evaluation across ten cultivar datasets against more
than fifteen methods, establishing that the adapter is
competitive with fully fine-tuned models while training
a small fraction of the parameters — 123× fewer
deployable parameters, summed across the ten datasets,
than the fully fine-tuned methods compared.
The adapter and its design ablation are therefore prior work,
summarized here as the architecture this article builds upon.
New to this article are:
1) the semantic adaptation warmup and its DAMAS augmentation, with the efficient two-layer attention formulation described above;
2) the measurement of the pretraining-to-cultivar gap reported above, which motivates the warmup and quantifies the problem the earlier paper left implicit;
3) ablations isolating the warmup’s components — its
augmentation, its objective, and the breadth of the corpus
it is trained on;
4) evidence that the warmup is independent of the adapter
it initializes: applied to a plain adapter with no interlayer module anywhere, it raises average accuracy by
8.8 points and helps on all ten datasets;
5) a comparison across pretraining strategies, showing the
warmup transfers between backbones; and
6) results at a second input resolution across all ten
datasets, together with insertion-location and downsampling-rate studies.

3

On the other side, self-supervised learning has also become
crucial to learn discriminative features with limited data [25]–
[27]. Foundational contrastive methods like SimCLR [34] and
SupCon [35] define positive pairs via data augmentations or
class labels, but their use of generic augmentations limits
performance in fine-grained tasks. Recent extensions, such as
PID [36] and SelfCon [37], introduce stronger cross-image
augmentations [38] or auxiliary modules that increase training
complexity or overlook subtle details critical for UFGIR.
Therefore, current SOTA methods such as MixViT [25],
CLE-ViT [26] and CSDNet [27] have incorporated selfsupervised learning to further enhance the extraction of ultrafine discriminative features; most recently, GAEor [39] sets
the fully fine-tuned state of the art on these benchmarks by
encoding geometric attributes of amplified details as a selfsupervised signal on a Swin-B backbone. However, they employ generic or weak data augmentation which limits feature
learning with scarce data. Additionally, most of these methods
employ transformer-based backbones with a large number of
parameters that all need to be stored for deployment and
require significant resources during training.
To address these limitations our method uses ViT attention
to identify and swap discriminative regions in image pairs
for label-aware contrastive learning. This approach enhances
representation learning under limited data constraints through
the usage of cross-image data-aware augmented positives.
Furthermore, we employ parameter-efficient transfer learning
to reduce number of deployable parameters and training costs,
ensuring our method remains scalable and deployment-friendly
while preserving ultra-fine-grained recognition performance.

II. R ELATED W ORK
A. Ultra Fine-Grained Image Recognition

B. Parameter-Efficient Transfer Learning

Ultra fine-grained image recognition (UFGIR) remains a
challenging task due to subtle inter-class differences and
limited labeled data. To address this, many methods build
on generic recognition backbones and augment them with
modules or loss functions that effectively select and aggregate discriminative features [29]–[32]. For instance, FFVT
[30] leverages ViT attention scores to extract and aggregate
intermediate low-, medium-, and high-level features through
the last transformer encoder block, enhancing sensitivity to
subtle fine-grained differences.

Parameter-efficient transfer learning (PETL) techniques seek
to fine-tune only a small subset of a model’s parameters while
keeping the majority of the pretrained backbone fixed. These
methods generally fall into two categories: prompt-tuning and
adapter-based approaches.

Given the data constraints in UFGIR, tailored data augmentation strategies [2], [31]–[33] are critical. Earlier methods such as SPARE [32] employ CNNs to extract semantic
regions for augmentation. More recent methods make use
of transformer aggregated attention for data-aware augmentations [23], [24], but incur high computational costs since
they employ attention rollout for aggregation [22] which has
complexity of O(L · N 3 ) with respect to the number of
transformer layers L and the sequence length N . In contrast,
our method uses an efficient implementation based on two
early ViT layers, drastically reducing complexity to O(N 2 ).

Prompt-tuning [13] augments the input sequence with additional task-specific, learnable tokens appended at various
stages of the transformer. For instance, VQT [40] employs
these tokens as queries to aggregate layer-wise information,
which is then integrated into the classification head. However,
the incorporation of extra tokens not only increases the computational cost during the forward pass but also leads to a
rapid increase in the parameters for the classification head.
In contrast, adapter-based methods insert lightweight, nonlinear modules directly within transformer layers. Originally
proposed by Houlsby et al. [21] in the natural language
processing domain, adapters have been successfully adapted
for vision transformers. For example, ConvPass [41] and ProTuning [14] extend this concept by integrating 2D convolutions to inject spatial biases, thereby enhancing the extraction

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

4

Fig. 3. Overview of the ViT architecture with our proposed Inter-Layer Adapter (ILA) and Semantic Adaptation Warmup (SAW) stage. The transformer
encoders are divided into three groups, with the ILA module applied after the first two groups to enforce hierarchical feature extraction via dual branch spatial
downsampling; this design enables residual gradient flow critical for incorporating downsampling in parameter-efficient setting. During the SAW stage, dualattention rollout from early layers guides data-aware augmentation through our Dual-Attention Mix Augmented SupCon (DAMAS) strategy, pretraining only
the adapter modules using a supervised contrastive loss. After warmup, the projection head is discarded and replaced by a classification head for fine-tuning
on the target task.

of fine-grained features. However, these existing PETL methods do not incorporate hierarchical biases, such as features
downsampling, which impacts the performance of UFGIR
tasks under data scarcity.

a classification head and train the model to predict the class
label based on a cross-entropy loss.

A. Vision Transformer Encoder
III. M ETHOD
The overview of our method is shown in Fig. 3. Our InterLayer Adapter (ILA) mitigates attention collapse [16]–[19] by
enforcing spatial hierarchical feature extraction via lightweight
adapters inserted between frozen ViT layers. The proposed
ILA employs two branches, Spatial Downsampling (SDS)
branch and Residual Spatial Downsampling (RSDS), to enable
residual gradient flow necessary for spatial downsampling in
the parameter-efficient setting.
Furthermore, to support better initialization for the adapter
modules we propose employing a Semantic Adaptation
Warmup (SAW) stage with Dual-Attention Mix Augmented
SupCon (DAMAS) strategy. During the SAW stage, dataaware cross-image mix augmentations are produced through
the use of an efficient dual-layer attention rollout mechanism.
Extracted features from the original images and augmented
are passed through a projection head to obtain representations
for a supervised contrastive loss. This loss enforces the model
to learn discriminative details to push apart representations
from different images and classes (negative pairs) and pull
closer images corresponding to the same class (positive pairs).
In this stage only the parameter-efficient modules are trained
while the encoder parameters are kept frozen. Subsequently,
in the finetuning stage we replace the projection head with

The Vision Transformer (ViT) encoder processes an input
image X ∈ RIS×IS×C , where IS denotes the image side
length and C the number of channels. The image is patchified
using a convolution with kernel size and stride P × P ,
producing SZ1 = IS/P patches along each spatial dimension.
These patches are flattened and linearly projected into Ddimensional embeddings, forming an input token sequence of
length N1 = SZ21 . A learnable classification token ([CLS])
is prepended to the sequence, and positional embeddings are
added to preserve spatial information. The resulting sequence
is then passed through a series of L transformer encoder
blocks, each consisting of multi-head self-attention (MHSA)
and position-wise feed-forward networks (PWFFN) [42]; the
output of each encoder block is denoted as Zl . Finally, a
LayerNorm [43] followed by a linear classifier is applied to
the [CLS] token for prediction.

B. Attention Collapse in Vision Transformers
Recent studies have identified a phenomenon known as
attention collapse in Vision Transformers (ViTs) [17], [19],
where attention weights between tokens become increasingly
uniform across layers. This over-smoothing [44] leads to loss
of high-frequency information and a reduction in representational diversity. Such uniformity is particularly detrimental in

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

ultra-fine-grained image recognition (UFGIR), where nuanced
inter-class variations must be preserved and emphasized.
The problem is further exacerbated in parameter-efficient
transfer learning (PETL) settings, where only a small number
of additional parameters (e.g., adapters) are fine-tuned while
the pretrained ViT backbone remains frozen. In such settings,
we observe that the attention matrices of deeper transformer
layers become extremely similar, as measured by the Centered
Kernel Alignment (CKA) similarity shown in Fig. 4. This
indicates redundancy in the feature encoding process, limiting
the ability of the model to extract discriminative features
necessary for fine-grained tasks and highlighting the need
for specialized mechanisms that preserve or enhance attention
diversity during adaptation.

5

C. ILA: Inter-Layer Adapter
To mitigate attention collapse and encourage the model
to attend to distinct spatial regions throughout the encoding
process, we introduce the Inter-Layer Adapter (ILA) module.
ILA explicitly imposes a hierarchical structure by progressively reducing the spatial resolution of token embeddings
between groups of transformer encoder blocks. This design
draws inspiration from prior work that embeds hierarchical
inductive biases into the model architecture for richer feature
encoding [45], [46].
We divide the transformer encoder into three groups, each
operating at a different spatial resolution. The spatial resolution at group i is denoted by SZi × SZi , where i ∈ {1, 2, 3}
and SZ1 > SZ2 > SZ3 . Transitions between these groups are
implemented using the ILA module, which performs spatial
downsampling according to:

SZi+1 = SZi − (KS − 1),

(1)

where KS is the kernel size of the depthwise convolution.
The token sequence zl ∈ RNi ×D at stage i (with Ni = SZ2i )
is first reshaped into a 2D feature map of shape SZi ×SZi ×D
to facilitate convolutional processing.

Fig. 4. Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) similarity [11] between attention
layers of a ViT after undergoing parameter-efficient fine-tuning on the Cotton
[2] dataset. Top-left: baseline without PETL modules. Top-right: ViT with
standard Adapters [21]. Bottom-left: ViT with the proposed Inter-Layer
Adapter (ILA). Bottom-right: ViT with ILA and the proposed SAW twostage training strategy. Lighter colors indicate higher inter-layer similarity,
reflecting increased redundancy. The top-left, top-right and bottom-left panels
are reproduced from our earlier workshop paper [1].

The ILA consists of two branches: the Spatial Downsampling (SDS) branch and the Residual Spatial Downsampling
(RSDS) branch. While the structure of the SDS resembles
the convolutional branch proposed in [41], our design introduces two key differences: (1) we use depthwise separable
convolution [47] to reduce computation, and (2) we omit
padding to explicitly reduce spatial resolution at each stage.
Furthermore, the RSDS branch provides a novel residual path
to preserve information across groups. These design choices
encourage feature diversity by enforcing hierarchical encoding
while reducing computational costs.
1) Spatial Downsampling Branch (SDS): The SDS branch
takes as input the token sequence Zl ∈ R(1+Ni )×D at group
i, where Ni = SZ2i , and performs spatial downsampling
through three main steps: (1) channel downsampling (CDS),
(2) depthwise separable convolution (DWSConv), and (3)
channel upsampling (CUS).
First, a projection reduces the channel dimension from D to
a smaller dimension d (d ≪ D), producing zCDS
∈ RNi ×d :

This design allows the RSDS branch to behave as an identity
function at the beginning of training when KS = 1, with
the capacity to adapt its weights to modulate the influence of
the original features, effectively acting as a learnable gating
mechanism [49] as training progresses. At the kernel sizes
actually deployed (KS > 1) the convolution at initialization
behaves as a sum-pooling operation instead, and it remains
trainable. Inspired by the transition from hand-crafted filters
to learnable ones in early convolutional networks such as
AlexNet [50], we adopt this strategy to introduce learnable
residual downsampling. This design provides richer inductive
bias than static methods while still retaining stability via nearidentity initialization.

2) Residual Spatial Downsampling (RSDS) branch: To
preserve local information and stabilize training by ensuring
gradient flow between encoder groups [48], we integrate a
learnable residual connection via the Residual Spatial Downsampling (RSDS) branch. This parallel path compensates for
the reduction in spatial resolution introduced by the SDS
branch. Unlike fixed pooling or interpolation which can erase
fine-grained spatial cues, RSDS uses a 2D depthwise convolution applied to the patch tokens only, leaving the [CLS] token
unchanged to preserve its global information.
Let zl ∈ R(1+Ni )×D denote the input token sequence,
where the first token corresponds to [CLS]. After removing
the [CLS] token and reshaping the remaining patch tokens
into a spatial feature map Zl ∈ RSZi ×SZi ×D , we apply a
depthwise convolution with the same kernel size KS as in
the SDS branch to each channel c ∈ {0, 1, . . . , D − 1}:

which is passed to the next encoder group. This additive
fusion allows the model to retain both the refined spatial
features from SDS and the residual identity-preserving path
from RSDS.

D. Semantic Adaptation Warmup (SAW) with DAMAS
To further address the challenges brought by data scarcity
in UFGIR, observed in Fig. 2 as overfitting to background
regions, we propose a Semantic Adaptation Warmup (SAW)
stage that allows our ViT with ILA modules to bridge the
semantic gap [28] between generic pretraining and ultra-finegrained recognition.
To address this issue we propose a novel strategy composed
of the three following components:
1) Data Diversity: we pool all ultra-fine-grained datasets to
create a rich, diverse corpus for the adaptation warmup.

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

7

Fig. 6. Example of our cross-image data augmentation. Discriminative regions
from original leaf images (left) are swapped between image pairs (right),
producing harder positive samples for ultra-fine-grained recognition.

2) Supervised Contrastive Warmup: we pretrain the
inter-layer adapters using a SupCon loss to align their
features with the frozen ViT backbone.
3) Early Attention Data-Aware Augmentation: Use
dual-attention rollout from two early layers to identify
and swap discriminative regions between image
pairs from the same label, generating diverse, labelaware, cross-image augmentations as positives for the
supervised contrastive loss.
After warmup, adapters are fine-tuned on the target dataset.
As shown in Fig. 4, incorporating this warmup stage results
in deeper transformer layers that capture more distinct and
discriminative features, in contrast to the collapsed representations observed with the frozen backbone alone. Figure 5
shows the same comparison on all ten datasets: averaged over
the deepest third of the encoder, similarity falls from 0.727
for the frozen encoder to 0.661 with adapters and 0.634 with
ILA, and the warmed state is lower still at 0.547.
We use the supervised contrastive loss [35] with a DualAttention-driven Mix Augmentation (DAMAS), since traditional data augmentation techniques apply generic transformations that often fail to capture the subtle, discriminative
details necessary for UFGIR. To generate label-aware positive
samples, we cut and swap discriminative regions, using the
dual-attention rollout, between image pairs from the same class
as seen in Fig. 6. To obtain the embedding r for the SupCon
loss, we first apply global average pooling to the feature ZL
produced by the final transformer block, and then pass the
resulting vector through a MLP projection head.

Fig. 7. Attention maps from aggregated layers (e.g., ”1–4” means layers 1
to 4) on UFGIR leaves datasets, showing the similarity between full attention
rollout (second row) and the proposed dual-attention approach (third and
fourth rows).

among image patches. Attention rollout [22] aggregates attention across layers to highlight regions of focus, and it has
been used for data augmentation [23], [24]. However, full
attention rollout over L layers is computationally expensive —
O(L · N 3 ) with full matrix products, and still O(L · N 2 ) when
only the [CLS] row is propagated — where N is the sequence
length and L is the number of aggregated layers, while relying
on a single layer often produces noisy or inconsistent maps
[51].
To address this, we propose a dual-attention strategy that
combines attention from two early layers, such as layers 2-3
or 3–4, as illustrated in Fig. 7. Specifically, we extract the
first row of the attention matrix (corresponding to the [CLS]
token) from one layer and multiply it with that of the next
layer, reducing the computation to O(N 2 ) — a factor of L
below a [CLS]-row rollout over all layers, and a factor of
L · N below the full-matrix form. The saving matters because
N grows quadratically with image resolution.
The third and fourth rows of Fig. 7 show that our dualattention maps capture discriminative areas comparable to the
full rollout (second row) while being much more efficient.
These discriminative regions guide our data-aware augmentation by swapping regions between image pairs (see Fig. 6),
enhancing fine-grained feature learning and data diversity for
UFGIR tasks.

In Tab. I we describe the datasets used for our experiments.
These are ultra-fine-grained leaves datasets collected by Yu et
al. [2], where each category represents a cultivar attached to
the seed obtained from the genetic resource bank.

over 3,526 cultivar labels, where the SoyAgeing subsets
share their parent’s label space — and contains no test
image of any dataset, verified by matching every image
path in the corpus against every dataset’s test split.

We conduct our experiments in two stages. First, we perform
a learning rate search over {0.003, 0.01, 0.03, 0.1, 0.3} using
a subset of the training data. In the second stage, we train the
model on the full training set using the selected learning rate
and evaluate performance on the test set over three independent runs. Results are reported in terms of top-1 accuracy mean
and standard deviation. When applicable, the best performance
values are highlighted in bold and the second-best values are
underlined.

To verify the effectiveness of the proposed approaches, we
compare against 13 SOTA methods and a linear-probe baseline
in the parameter-efficient setting. These methods are organized
into three Families based on their fine-tuning strategies and
feature aggregation mechanisms. The first category, PE Classifier, focuses on the classification head:

In addition, we assess computational efficiency by measuring 1) task-specific trainable parameters (TSP, the number of
parameters fine-tuned for the task), 2) inference floating-point
operations (FLOPs), and 3) inference throughput in terms of
images processed per second as measured on an NVIDIA RTX
3090. We use PyTorch and WandB [52] to implement and
manage experiments.
All models are optimized using Stochastic Gradient Descent
(SGD) with momentum of 0.9, a batch size of 8, and cosine
learning rate scheduler. We fine-tune models for 50 epochs. If
incorporating the SAW stage, we pretrain the PETL modules
once, for 100 epochs on the pooled corpus, and reuse that
checkpoint for every task; the warmup uses the same optimizer and batch size, a learning rate of 0.1, and a SupCon
temperature τ of 0.07.
For data preprocessing, we resize images to a square of size
300 × 300 or 600 × 600, then crop a random square during
training or a center crop during inference of size 224 × 224
or 448 × 448, respectively. All images are horizontally flipped
and normalized using ImageNet mean and standard deviation.
All experiments employ the ViT B-16 [10] backbone pretrained on ImageNet-21k, with a patch size P of 16, L = 12
transformer layers, and a hidden dimension D of 768. Reduced
adapter dimension d is set to 8. All methods fine-tune the
classification head and the [CLS] token. We propose four
different variations of our method:

ILA: inter-layer adapter modules with downsampling are
inserted by default after layers 4 and 8.
• ILA+: in addition to downsampling ILA modules, nondownsampling adapters with padding are inserted after
all layers except layer 4 and 8.
• ILA++: in addition to downsampling ILA modules, traditional intra-layer adapters [21] are added in every layer.
• SAW: extends ILA++ by integrating a semantic adaptation warmup stage, wherein our proposed Dual-Attentiondriven Mix Augmentation SupCon (DAMAS) strategy
pretrains the adapters on a diverse corpus of ultra-finegrained data before fine-tuning. The pooled corpus is the
union of the ten datasets’ training splits — 24,367 images

•

Linear Classifier (Baseline, ViT B-16): the simplest PE
method, which keeps the backbone frozen and, like every
method here, fine-tunes only the classification head and
the [CLS] token.
• Low-Rank Bilinear Pooling (LR-BLP) [53]: employs a
low-rank projection to reduce the dimensionality of the
bilinearly pooled features.
• Matrix Power Normalized Covariance (MPNC) [54],
[55]: applies covariance pooling of high-level features to
lessen instabilities of bilinear pooling.
• Intermediate Features Aggregation (IFA) [56]: selects
CLS tokens from intermediate layers and forwards them
through a small MLP to aggregate cross-layer features.
•

The next category includes FGIR methods where a module
explicitly select features based on some criteria, along with
a possible module to aggregate these selected discriminative
features. We evaluate these models in the parameter-efficient
setting where only a small percentage of modules are finetuned so we refer to them as PEFGIR. It includes:
CAL [57]: employs counter-factuality to train a bilinear
attention module, which is used for both pooling features
and to generate augmented versions of the input images.
The fine-tuned components include the attention module
and the bilinear attention pooling classification head.
• TransFG [29]: selects features from the previous-to-last
layer based on aggregated attention [22]. We fine-tune
the last transformer encoder block, where the feature
aggregation happens and the linear classification head.
• FFVT [30]: selects and aggregates intermediate features
based on layer-wise attention. The last transformer encoder block and the classification head are fine-tuned.
• RAMS-Trans (RAMS) [23]: crops the image for data
augmentation based on attention rollout [23]. Fine-tunes
only the classification head.
• GLSim [51]: computes the similarity between global and
local representations of an image to select crops. An
aggregator transformer block and the classification head
are fine-tuned.
•

The GRPETL category includes methods proposed for
generic image recognition under PETL settings:
•

VPT-Shallow (VPT-S) [13]: appends learnable prompts
before the transformer encoder.

• Visual Query Tuning (VQT) [40]: appends learnable

expedited towards the classification head where they are
concatenated into a single large dimensional linear layer.
• Adapter [21]: incorporates a small MLP inserted after the
MHSA and PWFFN of each transformer encoder block.
• ConvPass (ConvP) [41]: similar to the previous, it incorporates a small MLP inserted inside the transformer
block, but this MLP incorporates a 3 × 3 convolution in
between the channel downsampling and upsampling of
the adapter.

•

We also compare against the SOTA employing ViTs for
UFGIR published by Fang et al. [27]. These methods were
trained under traditional fine-tuning settings, with all of the
backbone parameters being adapted for each task so we refer
to them as FT. It includes:
SIM-Trans (SIM-Tr) [58]: employs structure information
and multi-level contrastive learning.
• CSDNet [27]: incorporates data-aware cropping augmentation, contrastive learning and self-distillation for learning with limited samples.
•

V. R ESULTS AND D ISCUSSION
A. The Pretraining-to-Cultivar Gap
Table V reports the measurement behind the motivation
given in Sec. I, dataset by dataset, on the pretrained encoder
with no training of any kind. Deep-layer CKA separates the

species benchmark from every cultivar dataset at both input
resolutions, and the two ranges do not overlap: 0.569 and 0.561
on CUB-200 against 0.709 to 0.743 across the ten. On cultivar
imagery the deepest third of the encoder has largely stopped
distinguishing one layer’s output from the next.
LogME needs more care, because it grows with the number
of classes. SoyGene and SoyGlobal score above CUB-200 on
it while carrying 1110 and 1938 classes against 200, so a direct
ranking across the column would be misleading. Restricted to
the seven cultivar datasets of comparable granularity, 198 to
200 classes, CUB-200 leads every one of them: 1.499 against
1.235 to 1.256. The same effect is visible in the SoyAgeing
rows, where the full set and its five subsets share 198 classes
and differ fivefold in size yet move LogME by at most 0.014.

B. Accuracy Comparison with State-of-the-Art
1) Evaluation at 224 × 224 Resolution: We evaluate the
effectiveness of our method across five UFGIR datasets at
a resolution of 224 × 224, as summarized in Tab. II. Our
variants provide consistent improvements over the baseline,
yielding gains between 13.5 points (ILA) and 27.4 points
(SAW) in average accuracy across datasets. This highlights
the effectiveness of incorporating hierarchical biases to address
attention collapse.
When combined with conventional adapters [21], the resulting ILA++ variant further improves performance, surpassing
the previous state-of-the-art, FFVT [30], by an average of
5.8 points. These results demonstrate the compatibility of the
proposed module with existing PETL mechanisms.
Incorporating the SAW stage for ILA++ leads to further
accuracy gains, most notably a 12.1-point improvement on the

2) Evaluation at 448 × 448 Resolution: To evaluate the

incorporating hierarchical feature extraction to increase discriminative feature recognition in UFGIR tasks.

This further validates the synergy of our hierarchical biases
with existing PETL modules.

After incorporating the SAW stage our model achieves the
best average results across all ten datasets among the compared
methods, surpassing the strongest fully fine-tuned method in
our comparison, CSDNet’s ViT-B variant [27], by 5.1 points.
Fully fine-tuned Swin-B models sit higher still — CSDNet’s
Swin-B variant and the recent GAEor [39] — at one to
two orders of magnitude more task-specific parameters; they
mark the accuracy ceiling of full fine-tuning rather than a
like-for-like comparison. Full fine-tuning still leads on three
datasets: SIM-Trans on SoyGlobal (70.69 against our 64.02)
and CSDNet on Cotton (57.92 against 55.42) and SoyGene
(70.82 against 70.53). Our model reaches its average while
deploying 123× fewer parameters across the ten datasets —
7.0 M of task-specific modules and heads against ten fully
fine-tuned CSDNet backbones — since their approach adapts
the whole backbone for each task. Part of SAW’s margin
reflects the pooled warmup corpus, which no competitor sees:
warmed up on the target dataset alone, SAW still gains 4.4
and 7.1 points over ILA++ on the two datasets probed in
Tab. XIII, with pooling contributing the remaining 1.7 and 4.9
points there. This highlights the strengths behind the proposed
semantic warmup with cross-image augmentations for efficient
adaptation.
Table VI makes the Swin comparison direct by deploying
our methods on the same Swin-B backbone at 448 × 448,
frozen, over three seeds. The ladder transfers: the warmup
adds 8.3 points over the adapter baseline and 14.9 over the
frozen head on average, reaching 74.87% — above our ViT
average of 73.5% — and it stabilizes the one dataset where
the adapter falls below the frozen head (SoyGlobal, 39.07%
with a 10.46 spread across seeds, restored to 64.61% with a
0.38 spread). Against full fine-tuning of the same backbone,
our result sits 0.8 points below CLE-ViT while topping it on
four of the ten datasets, and CSDNet and GAEor keep 4.2-

Fully fine-tuned Swin-B

Average

and 7.3-point average leads while adapting every backbone
parameter per task.
Additionally, these results demonstrate the scalability across
image resolutions of our proposed ILA variants. Our variants
improve the classification accuracy by at least 6.9 and up to
8.5 points on average across the ten datasets by increasing the
resolution from 224 to 448. This exceeds the mean improvement of 5.2 points across the rest of the methods, although
individual baselines come close (VPT-Deep gains 8.4 points).
These results combined with the reduced computational cost
of our method showcase the potential of our framework for
fine-grained tasks that require high-resolution.

C. Accuracy vs Computational Cost
We evaluate the trade-off between accuracy and computational cost across various PETL methods. In particular, we
compare the average top-1 accuracy across 10 UFGIR datasets
versus the computational cost in terms of task-specific trainable parameters, inference FLOPs and inference throughput.
We visualize these trade-offs in Fig. 8.
First, we highlight how the proposed ILA variants consistently increase the throughput and reduce the FLOPs compared
to other methods due to the reduction in processed tokens as a
consequence of the spatial downsampling. We also remark that
incorporating the Semantic Adaptation Warmup (SAW) stage
into ILA++ improves accuracy by 5.9% without increasing
task trainable parameters or inference cost. The warmup does
add training compute: 100 extra epochs, run once and shared

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

12

Fig. 8. Accuracy vs inference floating-point operations (FLOPs in log-scale, on the left) and throughput (images per second, on the right) for various PETL
methods. The y-axis indicates average top-1 accuracy across 10 UFGIR datasets, the x-axis shows inference floating-point operations (FLOPs) in log scale,
and the marker size reflects the number of task-specific trainable parameters.

by every task. This margin is not a longer-training effect.
Training ILA++ for 150 epochs per task, the same budget
spent without the warmup, raises its ten-dataset average from
67.7% to 69.2% over one seed, still 4.3 points short of SAW’s
73.5%.
The previous best PETL method, FFVT, achieves an average
accuracy of 60.9% while SAW reaches 73.5%, with 8%
fewer FLOPs, 40% higher throughput, and 93% fewer trained
parameters per task. Additionally, while the previous state-ofthe-art, CSDNet, achieves a competitive accuracy of 68.4%,
it deploys 123× more parameters across the ten datasets
since all backbone parameters are fine-tuned per task. Overall,
these results demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed
framework to improve recognition performance while reducing
processing costs in low-resource scenarios.

The closest prior art to the warmup is ExPLoRA [62], which
also inserts an extended pretraining stage on the target domain
before parameter-efficient fine-tuning. It differs in three ways
that matter here: it unfreezes one or two transformer blocks
where we train adapters only, it uses a self-supervised reconstruction or distillation objective where we use a supervised
contrastive one, and it augments generically where DAMAS
swaps discriminative regions between images of the same
class. The ExPLoRA runs here extend pretraining per dataset
— LoRA of rank 8 on the attention projections, 300 epochs
of masked-autoencoder reconstruction from the released MAE
checkpoint — and then fine-tune the same LoRA for 100
epochs at a learning rate of 10−3 over three seeds. Table VII
compares them on all ten datasets, alongside three alternative
pretrained backbones adapted the same way.
Extended pretraining helps: ExPLoRA raises the MAE
backbone from 22.51% to 50.11% on average and overtakes
both CLIP and DINOv2. It remains below our result on
every dataset, by 15.8 points on average. The warmup leads
every row except Cotton, where the DINOv2 backbone is

E. Ablations
1) Design of Residual Spatial Downsampling (RSDS)
Branch: We perform ablation experiments to evaluate the
importance of the Residual Spatial Downsampling (RSDS)
branch in our ILA++ framework. We observe that completely
removing the RSDS branch results in a collapsed representation, with accuracy dropping to near-zero levels, despite
the presence of both adapters and ILA. This confirms that

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

13

the RSDS branch residual signal propagation is essential for
enabling the overall architecture to learn.
Second, we examine the impact of removing spatial downsampling from the RSDS path by padding both the main and
residual branches to match dimensions. This configuration fails
to improve upon the baseline with adapters, highlighting the
need for inductive biases in the form of hierarchical feature
extraction.
Finally, we assess various design choices for the RSDS
operator. All alternatives fail to improve upon the adapter
baseline. In particular, depth-wise convolution with Xavier
initialization performs poorly, showing the importance of
initialization for proper residual propagation. In contrast, our
default design incorporating depth-wise convolution initialized
to near one values consistently outperforms all other variants, surpassing the adapter baseline by 8.5 and 8.0 points,
respectively. This confirms that a learnable, identity-preserving
initialization is essential for smoothly bridging the spatial mismatch introduced by downsampling while preserving feature
integrity.
These results validate the design choices made in the RSDS
as a necessary component within ILA, enabling the inclusion
of hierarchical biases for feature extraction in UFGIR.

2) Impact of ILA Insertion Position: We investigate the
effect of ILA placement within the backbone by evaluating
ILA++ with downsampling adapters inserted after various
layer pairs in Tab. IX. All placements improve mean accuracy
by at least 5.7 and up to 9.1 points, while reducing FLOPs

to as little as 62% of the original values, highlighting the
effectiveness of the proposed ILA in improving accuracy
by introducing hierarchical extraction while reducing costs.
Although placing adapters deeper (e.g., after layers 5 and
10) yields the overall highest accuracy, it also requires higher
FLOPs. Given this trade-off, we adopt the 4 & 8 configuration
as the default for ILA++, offering a strong balance between
efficiency and performance.

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

3) Impact of ILA Convolution Kernel Size: We study the
effects of the convolution kernel size within the ILA main and
residual branches, which directly controls the downsampling
rate in Tab. X. While employing a larger kernel size (e.g.,
kernel size 7) can improve accuracy in certain datasets and
reduce FLOPs due to the stronger downsampling ratio, the
kernel-7 configuration was not run on SoyGlobal, so its
behavior there is unmeasured. We opt for kernel size 3 due to
its stable training and balanced performance.
4) Impact of the Backbone Choice: To assess the effectiveness of the proposed methods across backbones, we deploy
them on DeiT [63] and DeiT3 [64] backbones in Tab. XI.
Results indicate that ILA++ and SAW consistently improve
upon the baseline regardless of the backbone choice. We
highlight how SAW can boost accuracy by up to 31.5 points
when combined with the DeiT3 backbone on the challenging
SoyGlobal dataset. These results demonstrate the effectiveness
of the proposed hierarchical feature extraction and semantic
warmup stage as robust techniques to distinguish ultra-fine
distinctions in parameter-efficient settings.

5) The Warmup Without ILA: Every warmup run reported
so far is built on ILA, so the warmup could be read as a
property of that module rather than a method in its own right.
Table XV settles this by applying it to a plain adapter with
no inter-layer module anywhere in the network. Average top1 accuracy rises from 53.42% to 62.20%, a gain of 8.78
points that holds on all ten datasets and ranges from 3.46 on
SoyAgeing to 15.34 on SoyLocal. The two arms are trained
identically at a fixed learning rate over three seeds, and differ
only in whether the warmup checkpoint is loaded.
The gain is larger than the 6.7 points the warmup adds on
top of ILA at the same 224 × 224 resolution, so the two are
complementary rather than one depending on the other. This is
what the framing in Sec. V-A predicts: the warmup addresses
a mismatch between the pretrained representation and cultivar
recognition, which is a property of the backbone and not of
the module placed on top of it.
6) Impact of Data Augmentation Strategy in SAW: We
compare data augmentation strategies to produce positives for
the contrastive loss function during the SAW stage in Tab. XII.
First, we remark how incorporating generic data augmentation
in the form of random cropping, color jittering, greyscale
or blur, as used in previous works [34], [35] can lead to
a significant performance degradation. This is because these
generic augmentations do not take into consideration the subtle
distinctions required for distinguishing fine-grained categories.
Second, incorporating data-aware cropping or masking [27],
[57], where an image region is zoomed in or substituted with
a value based on an attention mechanism, can be beneficial
for datasets with extreme data scarcity such as SoyLocal.
However, its benefits are limited compared to cross-image
data augmentation [38], [65] where attentive regions of two
images are swapped. This is because the additional diversity

SoyLocal
Average

+3.46
+6.46
+5.99
+8.79
+7.64
+8.12
+10.06
+12.36
+15.34
+8.78

introduced through swapping discriminative details between
two images from the same category allows the network to
directly learn from the large intra-class diversity observed in
UFGIR tasks.
7) Impact of Data Diversity in SAW Stage: We verify the
effect of data diversity during the warmup stage in Tab. XIII.
Our results show that incorporating the proposed DAMAS
significantly boosts the classification performance regardless
of if we warmup using a single-dataset before fine-tuning on
the same dataset or if we warmup using a combination of all
datasets before fine-tuning on each dataset individually. However, increasing data diversity by combining multiple datasets
is particularly effective at boosting performance on datasets
with very limited number of samples, such as SoyLocal,
where the proposed method employing a combined dataset can
further boost accuracy by 4.9%. This shows the importance of
data diversity during the warmup stage.
8) Impact of Loss Function used in SAW Stage: We
compare loss functions used during the adaptation stage in
Tab. XIV. SelfCon [37] incorporates representations from
intermediate layers of the network as additional positives for
the contrastive loss, resulting in a small increase in performance in SoyGlobal. However, processing these additional
positives increase the overall training cost so we opt to employ
SupCon [35], which employs only augmentations and sameclass images as positives, due its balance between accuracy
and cost.

IEEE TRANSACTIONS ON AGRIFOOD ELECTRONICS

VI. C ONCLUSION
This work presents a novel framework for parameterefficient ultra-fine-grained image recognition (UFGIR) that
combines a downsampling Inter-Layer Adapter (ILA) module
with Semantic Adaptation Warmup (SAW) stage. The ILA
module introduces inductive bias in the form of hierarchical
feature extraction for frozen Vision Transformers, significantly
reducing deployment parameters and inference cost. To address the semantic gap between generic pretraining and finegrained tasks we proposed SAW, an initialization strategy
that employs a novel Dual-Attention-driven Mix Augmentation
SupCon (DAMAS) objective to align adapter features with the
frozen backbone in a data-efficient manner.
The proposed framework incorporating ILA and SAW
achieves significant accuracy gains across ten UFGIR benchmarks while reducing inference cost and parameter overhead
compared to existing methods. These results highlight the
effectiveness of combining inductive biases with task-specific
pretraining for parameter-efficient adaptation in scenarios with
limited data.

=== PAPER: Decomposing Quantization Errors in Mandarin Speech Recognition ===

Received XX Month, XXXX; revised XX Month, XXXX; accepted XX Month, XXXX; Date of publication XX Month, XXXX; date of
current version XX Month, XXXX.
Digital Object Identifier 10.1109/XXXX.2022.1234567

Decomposing Quantization Errors in
Mandarin Speech Recognition
Edwin Arkel Rios1,3 , Augusto Christian Surya2,3 , and Bo-Cheng Lai1
1

National Yang Ming Chiao Tung University, Hsinchu City 300093, Taiwan
2
National Tsing Hua University, Hsinchu, Taiwan
3
Tranxform AI

This work was supported by Tranxform AI and NSTC under Grant 114-2221-E-A49-186. E. Rios and A. Surya conducted this work during an
are co-first authors.)

ABSTRACT Post-training quantization (PTQ) is a common route to deploying large automatic speech

recognition (ASR) models, yet its effect on Mandarin ASR is usually reported as a single character error
rate (CER), which hides how a model fails. We evaluated 19 models on 7 Mandarin dataset splits under
3 PTQ methods from 2 to 8 bits, measuring quantization cost in CER and decomposing errors into
linguistically meaningful components: tone and homophone confusions, and consonant and vowel errors
in the base syllable. Quantization robustness depends on model capacity within an architecture family but
does not transfer across families: at 3-bit, median ∆CER within Whisper ranges from +751.8 for Wh-T
to +1.1 for Wh-L, while the 24B Voxtral-S degrades by +34.2 compared with only +1.1 for the 1.5B
Wh-L, and the best BFloat16 model is not the most robust one. CER is strongly correlated with consonant
and vowel errors (ρ = 0.92 for both), more than with tone and homophone errors (ρ = 0.55 and 0.11).
Tone degradation nevertheless remains measurable: among syllables whose base pronunciation is correctly
recovered, tone errors increase consistently with quantization.
INDEX TERMS Automatic speech recognition, error analysis, post-training quantization.

I. Introduction

Automatic speech recognition (ASR) is a core technology
behind voice assistants, automatic transcription and speech
translation. Large multilingual models such as Whisper,
Qwen2-Audio, Qwen2.5-Omni and Voxtral [1]–[4] have
raised recognition accuracy across languages and domains,
and their quality is reported as a single error rate per
model. These models range from hundreds of millions to
tens of billions of parameters, and their memory and compute
requirements rule out edge devices and mobile hardware.
Post-training quantization (PTQ) is the usual solution. It
alleviates memory and inference cost without retraining, so
quantized models are available on more hardware. Its cost
for ASR has been measured mostly on English. Recent
PTQ studies benchmark Whisper and Moonshine on English
test sets [5], [6], and the one multilingual study finds that
quantization amplifies the penalty on smaller models and
under-represented languages [7]. All of them report that same

single error rate per model, which says how much a model
fails but not how.
Mandarin Chinese is the second largest language by number of speakers after English, with an estimated 1.2 billion
speakers in 2026 [8]. It is also a major language for digital
communication and voice-based applications, which makes
efficient Mandarin speech recognition relevant to a large and
geographically distributed user base. Mandarin gives a model
more ways to be wrong than English does. A character can be
wrong in its base syllable, through an initial or final error.
It can be wrong in its tone alone, with the base syllable
right. It can be wrong in the character alone, a homophone
with syllable and tone right. The character error rate (CER)
counts all three alike, so a single number cannot separate
them (Figure 1).
Work on Mandarin ASR error correction shows that pronunciation and homophone confusions dominate the errors of
full-precision models [9]–[11]. Compression is known to hit
rare, fine-grained distinctions first, in vision [12] and across

This work is licensed under a Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 License. For more information, see https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/

FIGURE 1. Ways a recognizer can be wrong. An English error changes
the base sound of a word. A Mandarin character can be wrong in its base
syllable (consonant or vowel), in tone alone, or in character alone
(homophone). CER counts all of these errors. Examples from Table 3.

the languages of quantized large language models [13].
Tone and homophone errors are therefore the natural first
casualties of quantization in Mandarin, and a single error rate
cannot show whether the three failure modes degrade alike.
This leads to a natural question: how does quantization
affect Mandarin speech recognition, and which part of
the syllable does it damage?
We evaluate quantized ASR and large audio-language
models (LALMs) on Mandarin short-form and long-form
speech, and decompose every error through pinyin. We
go one level deeper than prior pinyin-level analyses by
splitting the base syllable into its consonant and vowel. Our
contributions are:
• A study of quantized Mandarin ASR comprising 2,014
evaluation runs: 19 models, 7 quantization configuration, 7 Mandarin short-form splits and a 7-split longform corpus.
• The finding that quantization robustness follows model
capacity within an architecture family but not across
families, and not full-precision accuracy either. At 3bit the median ∆CER within Whisper runs from +751.8
for Wh-T to +1.1 for Wh-L, while the 24B Voxtral-S
degrades by +34.2 against +1.1 for the 1.5B Wh-L.
• A pinyin-level error analysis that decomposes recognition errors into consonant, vowel, tone and homophone components and locates quantization damage
in the base syllable. The Spearman rank correlation ρ
between CER and the consonant and vowel error rates
is 0.92 for both, against 0.55 for the conditional tone
error rate and 0.11 for homophones.
II. Related Work
A. Modern ASR Models

The models we evaluate span three classes. Whisper [1]
is an encoder-decoder trained on large-scale weakly supervised speech-text pairs, which made robust multilingual and
zero-shot recognition standard. Large audio-language models
(LALMs) such as Qwen2-Audio [2], Qwen2.5-Omni [3] and
Voxtral [4] feed an audio encoder into a large language
model and extend speech processing beyond transcription;
their decoders are the same networks whose quantization
the LLM literature studies. Efficiency-oriented variants such
as LiteASR [14], which compresses Whisper’s encoder by
low-rank approximation, push the accuracy-cost frontier and
2

are the natural companions of quantization on constrained
hardware. Section III-A lists the 19 checkpoints.
B. Mandarin ASR and Its Error Structure

Mandarin error analysis works through pinyin. The ASREC benchmark [9] is, to our knowledge, the first dedicated
benchmark for evaluating LLMs on Chinese ASR error
correction, built from the THCHS-30 [15], AISHELL-1 [16],
AISHELL-2 [17] and WenetSpeech [18] transcripts. Pinyinbased representations and regularization model pronunciation and homophone errors better, both for prompting and
fine-tuning correction LLMs [10] and for N-best rescoring
[11], and pinyin has been fed directly into an LLM-based
recognizer [19]. These works agree that Chinese homophones make substitution the dominant Mandarin error, and
they analyze the syllable through its initial and final, the
standard decomposition of Mandarin phonology [20].
They also share a limit. All of them target error correction
or model design and analyze full-precision models; none
asks how the error structure changes when the model’s
precision is cut. To our knowledge no prior work benchmarks
the interaction between PTQ and pinyin-level, let alone
consonant- and vowel-level, Mandarin error structure. That
is the question here.
C. Quantization of Speech Models

Quantization reduces the memory footprint and compute
cost of a network by storing its weights in fewer bits;
Gholami et al. [21] survey the field. We use three PTQ
toolkits: bitsandbytes [22], which implements LLM.int8()
[23] and NF4 [24], Quanto [25], and HQQ [26]. HQQ is
a calibration-free method in the optimization-based line that
GPTQ opened [27], the line that pushes usable bit-widths
below 4. The toolkits and bit-widths evaluated are described
in Section III-C.
For speech, PTQ has been benchmarked on Whisper and
Moonshine in English [5], [6] and combined with token
pruning for ASR hardware [28]; quantization-aware training
has been applied to audio source separation [29]. The one
multilingual study shows that quantizing Whisper amplifies
its penalty on smaller variants and under-represented languages [7]. All of these report an aggregate error rate.
Findings from LLM quantization set the expectations our
results test. Four-bit weight-only PTQ is close to the bit-level
optimum [30] and keeps performance comparable to full
precision [31], and robustness depends on training compute
and dynamics rather than on size alone [32], [33]. Across 1to 4-bit training, ParetoQ reports a sharp transition between
3 and 2 bits, below which the weight representations change
drastically [34]. We will meet the same transition in speech.
The damage is also uneven, which is where the tone
and homophone hypothesis comes from. Compression disproportionately affects long-tail, low-frequency classes in
vision [12] and disparately harms non-Latin-script and lowerresource languages under LLM quantization [13]. In machine
VOLUME ,

translation 2-bit PTQ hurts low-resource languages most
[35], and 4-bit losses grow on long inputs and non-English
text [36]; one k-quantization study reports no such gap
[37].Tone and homophone disambiguation are a fine-grained,
lower-frequency signal within Mandarin, which makes them
a reasonable candidate for disproportionate degradation.
None of these studies decomposes the error below the word
or character, and none covers a tonal language. This paper
does both, across 19 models and five bit-widths.

We evaluate on 7 Mandarin dataset splits from 5 corpora
(Table 2): Common Voice releases 15 and 19 [38], counted
as two corpora, AISHELL-1 [16], WenetSpeech [18] and
Taiwan-Tongues [39]. The long-form corpus of Section IVF uses HSK-Speech [40] and CMMLU-Speech [41]. The
English reference point of Section IV-A uses 23 English
splits from LibriSpeech [42], TED-LIUM [43], GigaSpeech
[44], VoxPopuli [45], Common Voice [38], the English
ASR benchmark of [46], and further meeting, financial and
spoken-question corpora.
C. Quantization Methods

We evaluate 6 post-training quantization (PTQ) configurations: bitsandbytes (BNB) [22] at 4-bit (NF4 [24]) and 8-bit
(LLM.int8() [23]), Quanto [25] at 8-bit, and HQQ [26] at
2-, 3-, and 4-bit precision. We denote these configurations
as BNB4, BNB8, Quanto8, HQQ2, HQQ3, and HQQ4,
respectively. HQQ quantizes each weight matrix group-wise;
we use its default group size of 64, with the per-group
scale and zero point kept in bfloat16 and not themselves
quantized. Both the group size and the precision of those
constants matter at 2 bits, so the HQQ2 results below are
specific to this configuration. For the LiteASR models the
integration quantizes the dense matrices only, leaving the
low-rank factors of each compressed layer in bfloat16.
D. Error Metrics

III. Experimental Setup
A. Models

Our experiments use 19 models, shown in Table 1, in four
families: Whisper, LiteASR, Qwen and Voxtral. Whisper
names give the size (T/B/S/M/L) or version (Lv2, Lv3), with
Lv3t the large-v3-turbo variant. LiteASR names carry a t-/lprefix for the teacher checkpoint (large-v3-turbo or large-v3)
and an -f/-a suffix for the fast/accurate variant, so the six
form two compression families rather than one size ladder.
Qwen2 and Qwen2-Ins are Qwen2-Audio-7B and its Instruct
variant; Voxtral-Mi and Voxtral-S are Voxtral-Mini-3B and
Voxtral-Small-24B.
VOLUME ,

In addition to CER, we evaluate several Chinese-specific
error metrics that decompose recognition errors into
pronunciation-related components. For each utterance, the
reference sequence is the ground-truth transcript, while the
hypothesis sequence is the model output. The two sequences
are aligned on their pinyin representations, with insertions
and deletions always counted as errors.
• Base-syllable error rate (BSER): word error rate on
base-pinyin (tone-stripped) syllable sequences, measuring syllable recognition independent of tone.
• Consonant error rate (CONER) and vowel error rate
(VER): word error rates computed independently on the
consonant (initial, C ) and vowel (final, V ) sequences
3

associated with larger degradation in another.

of the reference and hypothesis pinyin. This separates
base-syllable errors into consonant and vowel errors.
The split is the standard initial/final decomposition of
Mandarin phonology [20]; to our knowledge it has
not been used before to localize quantization damage.
Table 3 gives a worked example.
• Tone (TER) and homophone (HER) errors. These
errors are counted only for aligned character pairs
whose base pinyin matches. Let ri and hi denote
the i-th aligned reference and hypothesis characters,
respectively, when neither is an insertion or deletion;
N denote the number of reference characters; B(·)
denote the base pinyin (tone-stripped syllable) of a
character. Let M denote the number of aligned pairs
with matching base pinyin, T the number of these pairs
with a tone mismatch, and H the number with matching
base pinyin and tone but different characters:
M =

∑︁

i 1[Bi ∧ T (ri ) ̸= T (hi )]

∑︁

i 1[Bi ∧ T (ri )=T (hi ) ∧ ri ̸= hi ]

A base-pinyin mismatch is excluded from these counts
and is instead captured by CER and BSER. Thus, a
base matching pair can be classified as a tone error, a
homophone error, or a correct character.

8-bit quantization has little effect on CER. Figure 2 shows
only marginal CER changes at 8 bits: the median ∆CER
over the 19 models is 0.0 under Quanto8 and 0.1 under
BNB8. The one model that degrades by more than one point
is Qwen2-Ins under Quanto8 (+6.3).
4-bit quantization costs more than 8-bit but stays manageable. The increase at HQQ4 is visible in Figure 2 yet
small for most of the grid, and the damage is confined to
the smaller models: Wh-T, LiteASR-t and Wh-B degrade by
+20.3, +13.2 and +7.5 ∆CER, and the two Voxtral models
by +2.8 and +3.0. Of the remaining 14 models, 12 change
by less than one point (−0.1 to +0.9) and two, Qwen2Ins and LiteASR-tf, improve by 6.2 and 2.5. Under BNB4
the largest degradation is Qwen2-Ins at +181.6, followed by
Wh-T at +33.7.
3-bit quantization breaks the smaller models. At HQQ3
the smallest Whisper encoders collapse: Wh-T by +751.8
and Wh-B by +129.9 ∆CER (Table 7, Figure 3). The audiolanguage models also degrade sharply, Qwen2 by +106.6,
Voxtral-Mi by +53.1 and Voxtral-S by +34.2, while Wh-L
holds at +1.1 and Qwen2.5-Omni at +1.7.
2-bit quantization fails for most models. At HQQ2 every
model exceeds 20 CER and only 6 of 19 keep a median CER
below 100 (Figure 3). No audio-language model stays below
589 CER on any of the 7 dataset splits. Qwen2.5-Omni, the
best model at BFloat16, collapses to 1575.4. The survivors
are all encoder-decoder ASR models: 4 Whisper-family
models (Wh-L, LiteASR-la, LiteASR-lf and LiteASR-l) stay
below 100 on every split. The cliff between 3 and 2 bits is
the transition ParetoQ reports for LLM weights, which stay
near their pre-trained distribution down to 3 bits and change
drastically below [34]; PTQ has no retraining with which to
absorb that change.
B. Effect of Model Capacity on Quantization Robustness

IV. Results

Larger models are more robust within the same architecture family. Focusing on Whisper, whose checkpoints form
a clean capacity ladder, baseline CER falls with size along
the tiny-to-large ladder, from 44.1 for Wh-T to 7.7 for Wh-L
(Table 4). So does the HQQ3 penalty (Table 7): +751.8 for
Wh-T and +129.9 for Wh-B, flattening from Wh-S upward
at +3.9, +1.1 (Wh-L) and +1.5 (Wh-Lv3). The benefit of
capacity is largest among the small models.

We use 2 Spearman correlations to distinguish overall metric
relationships from quantization-induced changes. We denote
correlations between the metric levels themselves as ρ, which
measures whether two metrics are correlated across runs. In
contrast, ρ∆ denotes correlations between their changes from
BFloat16 to lower bits (Table 6), which measures whether

However, parameter count alone is not a reliable predictor across different architectures. The 24B Voxtral-S
degrades by +34.2 CER at 3-bit, far more than the 1.5B WhL at +1.1, even though Wh-L has the lower baseline CER
of the two (Table 4). Within one family size helps; between
families it is not a usable signal.

For tone errors, we report both TER = T /N , which
measures tone errors regardless of whether the base syllable
is correct, and TER - B = T /M , which measures tone errors
only when the base syllable is correct. We use TER for
severity comparisons and retain TER - B for comparison with
prior error-analysis work.

4

VOLUME ,

FIGURE 2. CER of 14 of the 19 models across four quantization levels (BFloat16, Quanto8, BNB8, HQQ4), grouped by family and ordered by ascending
parameter count within each. Models and conditions omitted here for axis readability remain in every statistic, all of which are computed on the
complete data. The 2-bit condition is shown in Figure 3.

Training compute separates checkpoints of one architecture. Wh-Lv3t has 809M parameters, close to Wh-M, yet
a BFloat16 CER of 11.8 against 10.8 and a ∆CER at HQQ3
of +18.3 against +1.2. This matches the LLM finding that
quantization robustness is set by training hyperparameters
and token count rather than parameter count alone [32], [33].
We see the same pattern among the three 1.5B Whisper
checkpoints, which share one architecture and differ only
in training (BFloat16 CER 7.7, 10.3 and 8.0), yet at HQQ2
Wh-L reaches 33.2, Wh-Lv2 57.4 and Wh-Lv3 149.1. Largev2 was trained for 2.5 times more epochs than large with
added regularization [47], and large-v3 on five million hours
of audio [48]. Robustness falls in the order of training
compute, as the precision scaling law predicts: post-training
quantization degradation grows with the amount of training
data [32].
Model weights do not explain quantization robustness.
We next asked whether the weights themselves say which
checkpoint will survive. Quantizing every weight to 2 bits
with one group-wise round-to-nearest scheme, we measured
two properties per checkpoint: the relative reconstruction
error, how far the quantized weights land from the originals,
and the excess kurtosis of the weight distribution, how heavy
its tails are and so how many outliers a fixed grid has to span.
Among the 1.5B Whisper checkpoints both run against the
outcome. Wh-L has the larger reconstruction error (23.0%
against 22.0% for Wh-Lv2) and the heavier tails (kurtosis
1.0 against 0.6), yet it is the more robust of the two at
2 bits. Within LiteASR the statistics do separate the two
teacher families: the large-v3-derived checkpoints, LiteASRl, LiteASR-la and LiteASR-lf, sit within 23.0% to 23.3% of
each other, while the turbo-derived LiteASR-t, LiteASR-ta
and LiteASR-tf carry heavier tails (kurtosis 3.4 to 4.0 against
1.5 to 1.8). Weight statistics alone therefore do not decide

models and configurations on 23 English splits and comparing the 18 models the two corpora share (Table 5).

The same weight error costs more in Mandarin. At
HQQ3, 17 of 18 models keep a median CER below 100
in English against 13 in Mandarin, and 16 of 18 lose more
in Mandarin: HQQ3 multiplies CER by a median ×1.14 in
English and ×1.88 in Mandarin. At HQQ2 the multipliers
compress for models whose Mandarin baseline is already
poor, so we restrict the comparison to the 6 models that
stay below 100 in both languages; every one of them is
hit harder in Mandarin, by a median ×2.4 against ×4.3.
The two languages still agree on which checkpoint to pick
under quantization: across the 18 models the English and
Mandarin HQQ2 medians rank together at ρ = 0.89, where
their BFloat16 medians agree at only ρ = 0.39.
Capacity orders the models the same way in both
languages. The ladder of Section IV-B holds in English
as in Mandarin: BFloat16 CER falls from Wh-T to the 1.5B
checkpoints in both columns of Table 5, and the smallest
encoders are the first to break in both. What changes is
the size of the gap between the languages, which narrows
monotonically along the tiny-to-large ladder, from ×4.4 for
Wh-T to ×1.6 for Wh-L. Capacity therefore buys more in
Mandarin than in English, which is consistent with Mandarin
being the harder of the two targets for a fixed budget rather
than a different problem.
The pretraining mix sets the Mandarin baseline, not the
robustness. The two Chinese-developed Qwen models have
the strongest Mandarin baselines of the grid: Qwen2.5-Omni
is the best of the 18 at 4.7 CER against 2.1 in English, the
narrowest gap between the languages of any model except
Wh-L (×2.3 against ×1.6). The LiteASR compressions run
the other way: 5 of the six variants open a wider gap between
the languages than the checkpoint they were compressed
from, reaching ×16.0 for LiteASR-t and ×16.1 for LiteASRtf against ×5.3 for their teacher, so what the compression

Robustness does not follow the same ordering. Qwen2 loses
more in Mandarin relative to English at HQQ3 than any
other model (×8.2), and at HQQ2 both Qwen models exceed
